# Moon

This article is about Earth's natural satellite. For moons in general, see [Natural satellite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_satellite). For other uses, see [Moon (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_\(disambiguation\)).

The **Moon** is the only [natural satellite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_satellite) of [Earth](9228.md). It [orbits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbit_of_the_Moon) Earth at an average [distance of](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_distance) 384,399 kilometers (238,854 mi). Because the Moon follows an [elliptical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elliptic_curve) path, its distance from Earth varies over the year, ranging from 357,000–407,000 kilometers (222,000–253,000 mi),[^cite_note-NSSDC-2] a distance roughly 30 times the [width of Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_diameter). It [completes an orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_period) ([lunar month](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_month)) in relation to Earth and the [Sun](26751.md) ([synodically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunation)) every 29.5 days. The Moon and Earth are bound by [gravitational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitational) attraction, which is stronger on the sides facing each other. The resulting [tidal forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_force) are the main driver of Earth's [tides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tide), and have pulled the Moon to always face Earth with the same [near side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_side_of_the_Moon). This [tidal locking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_locking) effectively synchronizes the Moon's [rotation period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotation_period_\(astronomy\)) ([lunar day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_day)) to its orbital period (lunar month).

In [geophysical terms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geophysical_definition_of_planet), the Moon is a [planetary-mass object](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary-mass_object) or [satellite planet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_planet). Its mass is 1.2% that of the Earth, and its diameter is 3,474 km (2,159 mi), roughly one-quarter of Earth's (about as wide as the [contiguous United States](83759.md)). Within the [Solar System](26903.md), it is [larger and more massive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Solar_System_objects_by_size) than any known [dwarf planet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwarf_planet), and the fifth-largest and fifth–most massive moon, as well as the largest and most massive in relation to its [parent planet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_body).[^cite_note-Metzger2021-22] Its [surface gravity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surface_gravity) is about one-sixth of Earth's, about half that of [Mars](14640471.md), and the second-highest among all moons in the Solar System after [Jupiter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jupiter)'s moon [Io](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Io_\(moon\)). The body of the Moon is [differentiated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_differentiation) and [terrestrial](48510.md), with only a minuscule [hydrosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrosphere), [atmosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmosphere_of_the_Moon), and [magnetic field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetic_field_of_the_Moon). The [lunar surface](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_surface) is covered in [regolith dust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_regolith), which mainly consists of the fine [material ejected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ejecta_blanket) from the [lunar crust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_crust) by [impact events](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_event). The lunar crust is marked by [impact craters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_crater); some younger ones feature bright [ray-like streaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ray_system). The Moon was volcanically active until 1.2 billion years ago, surfacing lava mostly on the thinner near side of the Moon, filling ancient craters, which through cooling formed the today prominently visible dark plains of [basalt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basalt) called [maria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_mare) ('seas'). The [origin of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origin_of_the_Moon) is not clear, although it is thought to have been formed out of material from Earth that was ejected by [a giant impact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giant-impact_hypothesis) of a [Mars-sized body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theia_\(planet\)) 4.51 billion years ago, not long after [Earth's formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Earth).

From a distance, the day and night phases of the lunar day are visible as the [lunar phases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_phases), and when the Moon passes through [Earth's shadow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth's_shadow) a [lunar eclipse](18298.md) is observable. The Moon's [apparent size](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apparent_size) in Earth's sky is about the same as that of the Sun, which causes it to cover the Sun completely during a total [solar eclipse](5024887.md). The Moon is the [brightest celestial object](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_brightest_natural_objects_in_the_sky) in Earth's [night sky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night_sky) because of its large apparent size, while the [reflectance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reflectance) (albedo) of its surface is comparable to that of [asphalt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asphalt_concrete). About 59% of the surface of the Moon is visible from Earth owing to the different angles at which the Moon can appear in Earth's sky ([libration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libration)), making parts of the [far side of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon) visible.

The Moon has been an important source of inspiration and knowledge in human history, having been crucial to [cosmography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmography), [mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mythology), [religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_deity), art, [time keeping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_calendar), [natural science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_science) and [spaceflight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spaceflight). The [first spaceflights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Solar_System_exploration) to an extraterrestrial body were to the Moon, starting in 1959 with the [flyby](48936466.md) of *[Luna 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_1)* (sent by the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union)), and the [intentional impact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impactor_\(spacecraft\)) of *[Luna 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_2)*, followed in 1966 by the first [soft landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_landing) (by *[Luna 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_9)*) and [orbital insertion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_insertion) (by *[Luna 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_10)*). Humans first arrived in orbit with [Apollo 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_8) (sent by the [United States](3434750.md)) on December 24, 1968, and then on the surface with [Apollo 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_11) on July 20, 1969, making the Moon the only celestial body beyond Earth that humans have visited. By 1972, six Apollo missions had landed [twelve people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_who_have_flown_to_the_Moon#People_who_walked_on_the_Moon) on the Moon and stayed up to three days. Renewed robotic [exploration of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exploration_of_the_Moon), in particular to confirm the presence of [water on the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_water), has fueled plans to return humans to the Moon. These plans include the [Artemis program](60758751.md), which included the [Artemis II](41391511.md) crewed lunar flyby in 2026.

## Names and etymology

The English proper name for Earth's natural satellite is typically written as *Moon*, with a capital *M*.[^cite_note-23][^cite_note-PN-FAQ-24] The noun *moon* is derived from [Old English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_English) *mōna*, which stems from \**mēnōn*,[^cite_note-25] which in turn comes from [Proto-Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Indo-European) \**mēnsis* ('month')[^cite_note-26] – from earlier \**mēnōt* (genitive \**mēneses*), which may be related to a verb meaning 'to measure \[time]'.[^cite_note-barnhart1995-27]

The Latin name for the Moon is *lūna*. The English adjective *lunar* was ultimately borrowed from Latin, likely through French. In scientific writing and science fiction, the Moon is sometimes referred to as *Luna* [/ˈluːnə/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English)[^cite_note-28] to distinguish it from other moons. In poetry, *Luna* may also refer to the [personification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personification) of the Moon as a woman.[^cite_note-OEDLuna-29]

The Ancient Greek word *selḗnē* referred to the Moon as a celestial body, and also to the moon goddess [Selene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selene) [/səˈliːniː/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English). The rare English adjective *selenian* [/səliːniən/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English)[^cite_note-30] is used to describe the Moon as a world, as opposed to a celestial object.[^cite_note-31] Its cognate *selenic*, originally a rare synonym,[^cite_note-32] now almost always refers to the chemical element [selenium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selenium).[^cite_note-33] The corresponding prefix *seleno-* appears in terms including *[selenography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selenography)* (the study of the lunar surface).[^cite_note-oed-34][^cite_note-35]

[Artemis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis), the Greek goddess of the wilderness and the hunt, also came to be identified with Selene, and was sometimes called [Cynthia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cynthia) after her birthplace on [Mount Cynthus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Cynthus).[^cite_note-Pannen2010-36] Her Roman equivalent is [Diana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_\(mythology\)).[^cite_note-carlsen-37]

The [astronomical symbols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomical_symbols) for the Moon are the crescent ☽ and decrescent ☾,[^cite_note-38] for example in *M*<sub>☾</sub> 'lunar mass'.

### Classification

The International Astronomical Union (IAU) calls Earth's satellite "the Moon", with a capital "M".[^cite_note-39] Other natural satellites of planets are called 'moons', with a lower-case "m".[^cite_note-40] The Moon has historically been identified as a [classical planet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_planet), in the original sense of a wandering object in the sky. When Galileo discovered satellites orbiting Jupiter, he named them 'moons' because, like the Moon, they can be considered planets orbiting another planet.[^cite_note-k502-41]<sup>: 7 </sup> Some experts hold to a [geophysical definition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geophysical_definition_of_planet) that classifies moons as planets that orbit the Sun while incidentally orbiting another planet.[^cite_note-k502-41]<sup>: 25 </sup>

In a [heliocentric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heliocentrism#Modern_use_of_geocentric_and_heliocentric) [frame of reference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frame_of_reference) the Moon orbits the Sun,[^cite_note-q529-42] [perturbed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perturbation_\(astronomy\)) by Earth.[^cite_note-y136-43] This has led some scientists to argue that the Moon could be identified as a planet, both historically[^cite_note-k502-41] and qualitatively, adding that its mass would be enough to clear its orbit around the Sun if it were on its own.[^cite_note-s592-44] Some have suggested that this implies that the Earth–Moon system is a [double planet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_planet).[^cite_note-els-45]<sup>: 70 </sup> The [International Astronomical Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Astronomical_Union) standards organization has no definition for planets in a [binary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binary_system), or for what constitutes a double planet system, but most scientists agree that this would require the Moon-Earth [barycenter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barycenter_\(astronomy\)) to be outside of Earth, which it is not.[^cite_note-l982-46]

## Natural history

### Formation

Main articles: [Origin of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origin_of_the_Moon) and [Giant-impact hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giant-impact_hypothesis)



This not-to-scale animation shows (in an Earth and Sun stationary rotating frame of reference) the formation of the Moon through the collision of the hypothetical Mars-sized planet named [Theia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theia_\(hypothetical_planet\)) with Earth. After orbiting for tens of millions of years at the L

4

&#x20;[Lagrange point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagrange_point) as an Earth-[trojan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trojan_\(astronomy\)), Theia's orbit comes in "loops" finally too close to Earth.

[Isotope dating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotope_dating) of lunar samples suggests the Moon formed around 50 million years after the [origin of the Solar System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origin_of_the_Solar_System).[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-Age-48] Historically, several formation mechanisms have been proposed,[^cite_note-49] but none satisfactorily explains the features of the Earth–Moon system. A fission of the Moon from Earth's crust through [centrifugal force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centrifugal_force)[^cite_note-Binder-50] would require too great an initial rotation rate of Earth.[^cite_note-BotM-51] Gravitational capture of a pre-formed Moon[^cite_note-Mitler-52] depends on an unfeasibly extended [atmosphere of Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmosphere_of_Earth) to [dissipate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissipate) the energy of the passing Moon.[^cite_note-BotM-51] A co-formation of Earth and the Moon together in the [primordial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumplanetary_disk) [accretion disk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accretion_disk) does not explain the depletion of metals in the Moon.[^cite_note-BotM-51] None of these hypotheses can account for the high [angular momentum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angular_momentum) of the Earth–Moon system.[^cite_note-53]

The prevailing theory is that the Earth–Moon system formed after a [giant impact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giant_impact) of a [Mars](14640471.md)-sized body (named *[Theia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theia_\(planet\))*) with the [proto-Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Earth). The oblique impact blasted material into orbit about the Earth and the material accreted and formed the Moon[^cite_note-taylor1998-54][^cite_note-55] just beyond the Earth's [Roche limit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roche_limit) of \~2.56 [*R*<sub>🜨</sub>](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_radius).[^cite_note-56]

Giant impacts are thought to have been common in the early Solar System. Computer simulations of giant impacts have produced results that are consistent with the mass of the lunar core and the angular momentum of the Earth–Moon system. These simulations show that most of the Moon derived from the impactor, rather than the proto-Earth.[^cite_note-57] However, models from 2007 and later suggest a larger fraction of the Moon derived from the proto-Earth.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59][^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61] Other bodies of the inner Solar System such as Mars and [Vesta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/4_Vesta) have, according to meteorites from them, very different oxygen and tungsten [isotopic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotope) compositions compared to Earth.[^cite_note-62] However, Earth and the Moon have nearly identical isotopic compositions. The isotopic equalization of the Earth–Moon system might be explained by the post-impact mixing of the vaporized material that formed the two,[^cite_note-Pahlevan2007-63] although this is debated.[^cite_note-64]

The impact would have released enough energy to liquefy both the ejecta and the Earth's crust, forming a magma ocean. The liquefied ejecta could have then re-accreted into the Earth–Moon system.[^cite_note-Warren1985-65][^cite_note-66] The newly formed Moon would have had [its own magma ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_magma_ocean); its depth is estimated from about 500 km (300 miles) to 1,737 km (1,079 miles).[^cite_note-Warren1985-65]

While the giant-impact theory explains many lines of evidence, some questions are still unresolved, most of which involve the Moon's composition.[^cite_note-67] Models that have the Moon acquiring a significant amount of the proto-Earth are more difficult to reconcile with geochemical data for the isotopes of zirconium, oxygen, silicon, and other elements.[^cite_note-68] A study published in 2022, using high-resolution simulations (up to 10<sup>8</sup> particles), found that giant impacts can immediately place a satellite with similar mass and iron content to the Moon into orbit far outside Earth's [Roche limit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roche_limit). Even satellites that initially pass within the Roche limit can reliably and predictably survive, by being partially stripped and then torqued onto wider, stable orbits.[^cite_note-69]

On November 1, 2023, scientists reported that, according to computer simulations, remnants of Theia could still be present inside the Earth.[^cite_note-NYT-20231101-70][^cite_note-NAT-20231101-71]

### Natural development



Artist's depiction of the Moon in Earth's sky as it might have appeared around 4 billion years ago, after the [Late Heavy Bombardment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Heavy_Bombardment). At that time the Moon orbited the Earth at a third of its current distance, making it appear 2.8 times larger in [apparent size](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apparent_size) than it does today.

[^cite_note-Lunar_and_Planetary_Institute-72]



The Moon was volcanically active for billions of years after its formation. Lava flooding the lunar low lying terrane over time formed today's basalt maria, with their ages marked in this map (yellow to red: youngest; blue to purple: oldest).

The newly formed Moon settled into a much closer Earth orbit than it has today. Each body therefore appeared much larger in the sky of the other, [eclipses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eclipse) were more frequent, and [tidal effects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_effect) were stronger.[^cite_note-Lunar_and_Planetary_Institute-72] Due to [tidal acceleration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_acceleration), the Moon's orbit around Earth has become significantly larger, with a longer period.[^cite_note-73]

Following formation, the Moon has cooled and most of [its atmosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmosphere_of_the_Moon) has been stripped.[^cite_note-John_2017-74] The [lunar surface](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surface_of_the_Moon) has since been shaped by large [impact events](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_event) and many small ones, forming a landscape featuring [craters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_crater) of all ages.

The Moon was [volcanically active](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanism) until 1.2 billion years ago, which laid down the prominent [lunar maria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_maria). Most of the [mare basalts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_mare) erupted during the [Imbrian period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imbrian), 3.3–3.7 billion years ago, though some are as young as 1.2 billion years[^cite_note-Hiesinger-75] and some as old as 4.2 billion years.[^cite_note-Papike-76] The distribution of the mare basalts is uneven, with the basalts predominantly appearing on the Moon's near-side hemisphere. The reasons for this are not yet known, although the relative thinness of the crust on the near side of the Moon is hypothesized to be a factor.[^cite_note-77] Causes of the distribution of the [lunar highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_highlands) on the [far side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon) are also not well understood. Topological measurements show the crust on the near side to be thinner than on the far side. One possible explanation for this is that large impacts on the near side may have made it easier for lava to flow onto the surface.[^cite_note-ESA_Science_&_Technology_2006-78]

### Lunar geologic timescale

Main article: [Lunar geologic timescale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_geologic_timescale)



Millions of years before present

The [lunar geologic timescale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_geologic_timescale) is based on the most prominent impact events, such as multi-ring formations like [Nectaris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nectarian), [Imbrium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Imbrian), and [Orientale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Orientale) that are between hundreds and thousands of kilometers in diameter and associated with a broad apron of ejecta deposits that form a regional [stratigraphic horizon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stratigraphy).[^cite_note-geologic-79] The lunar geological periods are named after their characteristic features, from most [impact craters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_crater) outside the dark [mare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_mare), to the mare and later craters, and finally the young, still bright and therefore readily visible craters with [ray systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ray_system) like [Copernicus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copernicus_\(lunar_crater\)) or [Tycho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tycho_\(lunar_crater\)).[^cite_note-80] The lack of an atmosphere, weather, and recent geological processes mean that many of these craters are well-preserved. [^cite_note-81] Although only a few [multi-ring basins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-ring_basins) have been definitively dated, they are useful for assigning relative ages. Because impact craters accumulate at a nearly constant rate, counting the number of craters per unit area can be used to estimate the age of the surface.[^cite_note-geologic-79] However care needs to be exercised with the [crater counting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crater_counting) technique due to the potential presence of [secondary craters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_crater). Ejecta from impacts can create secondary craters that often appear in clusters or chains but can also occur as isolated formations at a considerable distance from the impact. These can resemble primary craters, and may even dominate small crater populations, so their unidentified presence can distort age estimates.[^cite_note-82]

The radiometric ages of impact-melted rocks collected during the [Apollo missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_missions) cluster between 3.8 and 4.1 billion years old: this has been used to propose a [Late Heavy Bombardment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Heavy_Bombardment) period of increased impacts.[^cite_note-83]

High-resolution images from the Lunar Reconnaissance Orbiter in the 2010s show a contemporary crater-production rate significantly higher than was previously estimated. A secondary cratering process caused by [distal ejecta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distal_ejecta) is thought to churn the top two centimeters of regolith on a timescale of 81,000 years.[^cite_note-84][^cite_note-85] This rate is 100 times faster than the rate computed from models based solely on direct micrometeorite impacts.[^cite_note-86]

### Future

See also: [Destruction of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Destruction_of_the_Moon)

In five billion years the Moon will have wandered 40% further away from Earth than it is now.[^cite_note-87] However, about two to three billion years after that, the Sun will have become a [red giant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_giant). Assuming the Sun envelops the Earth–Moon system, the consequent drag from the Sun's atmosphere may cause the orbital distance between the Earth and the Moon to decay to the point where the Moon comes within the Earth's [Roche limit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roche_limit), leading it to disintegrate.[^cite_note-f170-88]

## Physical characteristics

Main article: [Geology of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_the_Moon)

### Size and mass

Further information: [List of natural satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_natural_satellites)



Size comparison of the main moons of the Solar System with Earth to scale. Nineteen moons are large enough [to be round](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrostatic_equilibrium), several having [subsurface oceans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsurface_ocean) and one, [Titan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titan_\(moon\)), having a considerable atmosphere.

The Moon is the fifth largest (by size and mass) natural satellite of the Solar System. It is categorizable as a [planetary-mass moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary-mass_moon), making it a satellite planet under the [geophysical definitions of the term](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geophysical_definition_of_planet).[^cite_note-Metzger2021-22] It is smaller than [Mercury](19694.md) but considerably larger than the largest [dwarf planet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwarf_planet) of the Solar System, [Pluto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluto). The Moon is the second-densest satellite in the Solar System, after [Io](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Io_\(moon\)).[^cite_note-Schubert2004-89]

With a mass of 1⁄81 of Earth's,[^cite_note-worldbook-90] and a diameter about 3,500 km (more than one-quarter of Earth's), the Moon is the largest natural satellite in the Solar System relative to its [primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_\(astronomy\)) planet.[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-Moon_vs._Charon-92][^cite_note-93] The face of the Moon is comparable to the width of [mainland Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland_Australia),[^cite_note-Australia-94] Europe or the [contiguous United States](83759.md).[^cite_note-Dyches_2021_d923-95] The whole surface area of the Moon is about 38 million square kilometers, comparable to that of the whole [Americas](29833.md),[^cite_note-y857-96] the areas of the lunar hemispheres being comparable to the areas of [North America](21139.md) and [South America](26769.md).

### Shape

The Moon was initially in [hydrostatic equilibrium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrostatic_equilibrium) but has since departed from this condition.[^cite_note-97] It is a very slightly scalene [ellipsoid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellipsoid) due to tidal stretching, with its long axis displaced 30° from facing the Earth, due to gravitational anomalies from impact basins.[^cite_note-98] Its shape is more elongated than current tidal forces can account for. This 'fossil bulge' indicates that the Moon solidified when it orbited at half its current distance to the Earth, and that it is now too cold for its shape to restore hydrostatic equilibrium at its current orbital distance.[^cite_note-99] Today tidal crust deformation is limited to lobate thrust fault scarps formation.[^cite_note-c293-100]

### Structure

Main article: [Internal structure of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_structure_of_the_Moon)



Moon's internal structure: solid inner core (iron-metallic), molten outer core, hardened mantle and crust. The crust on the Moon's near side permanently facing Earth is thinner, featuring larger areas flooded by material of the once molten mantle forming today's [lunar maria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_mare).

The Moon is a [differentiated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_differentiation) body with a [geochemically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geochemically) distinct [crust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crust_\(geology\)), [mantle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mantle_\(geology\)), and [core](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_core). It a solid iron-rich inner core with a radius possibly as small as 240 kilometers (150 mi) and a fluid outer core primarily made of liquid iron with a radius of roughly 300 kilometers (190 mi). Around the core is a partially molten boundary layer with a radius of about 500 kilometers (310 mi).[^cite_note-101][^cite_note-Weber2011-102] This structure is thought to have developed through the [fractional crystallization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fractional_crystallization_\(geology\)) of a global magma ocean shortly after the Moon's formation 4.5 billion years ago.[^cite_note-103]

Crystallization of this magma ocean would have created a [mafic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mafic) mantle from the [precipitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precipitation_\(chemistry\)) and sinking of the minerals [olivine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olivine), [clinopyroxene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinopyroxene), and [orthopyroxene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthopyroxene); after about three-quarters of the magma ocean had crystallized, lower-density [plagioclase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plagioclase) minerals could form and float into a crust atop.[^cite_note-S06-104] The final liquids to crystallize would have been initially sandwiched between the crust and mantle, with a high abundance of [incompatible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compatibility_\(geochemistry\)) and heat-producing elements.[^cite_note-W06-1] Consistent with this perspective, geochemical mapping made from orbit suggests a crust of mostly [anorthosite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anorthosite).[^cite_note-L06-20] The [Moon rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_rock) samples of the flood lavas that erupted onto the surface from partial melting in the mantle confirm the mafic mantle composition, which is more iron-rich than that of Earth.[^cite_note-W06-1] The crust is on average about 50 kilometers (31 mi) thick.[^cite_note-W06-1]

However, the inner core of the Moon is small, with a radius of about 350 kilometers (220 mi) or less,[^cite_note-W06-1] around 20% of the radius of the Moon. Its composition is not well understood but is probably metallic iron alloyed with a small amount of sulfur and nickel. Analyses of the Moon's time-variable rotation suggest that it is at least partly molten.[^cite_note-105] The pressure at the lunar core is estimated to be 5 GPa (49,000 atm).[^cite_note-106]

### Gravitational field

See also: [Lunar orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_orbit)



Astronaut [John Young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Young_\(astronaut\)) jumping on the Moon, illustrating that the [gravitational pull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitational_acceleration) of the Moon is approximately 1/6 of Earth's. The jumping height is limited by the combined weight of Young and his EVA space suit on the Moon of about 27 kg (60 lb) and by the suit's pressurization resisting the bending of the suit, as needed for jumping.

[^cite_note-Kluger_2018_z081-107]

[^cite_note-Nast_2013_v237-108]

On average the Moon's [surface gravity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surface_gravity) is 1.62 [m/s<sup>2</sup>](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metre_per_second_squared)[^cite_note-NSSDC-2] (0.1654 [g](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G-force); 5.318 [ft/s<sup>2</sup>](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foot_per_second_squared)), about half of the [surface gravity of Mars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravity_of_Mars) and about a sixth of [Earth's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravity_of_Earth). Among the planetary moons, it has the second highest surface gravity, after [Io](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Io_\(moon\)). It has an escape velocity of 2.38 km/s (8600 km/h; 5300 mph).

The [Moon's gravitational field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitation_of_the_Moon) is not uniform. The details of the gravitational field have been measured through tracking the [Doppler shift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doppler_shift) of radio signals emitted by [orbiting spacecraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_orbiter). The main lunar gravity features are [mascons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_concentration_\(astronomy\)), large positive gravitational anomalies associated with some of the giant impact basins, partly caused by the dense mare basaltic lava flows that fill those basins.[^cite_note-109][^cite_note-110] The anomalies greatly influence the [orbit of spacecraft about the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_orbit). There are some puzzles: lava flows by themselves cannot explain all of the gravitational signature, and some mascons exist that are not linked to mare volcanism.[^cite_note-111]

The [sphere of influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphere_of_influence_\(astrodynamics\)) of the Moon's gravity field, in which it dominates over Earth's, has a [Hill radius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hill_sphere) of 60,000 km (i.e., extending less than one-sixth the distance of the 378,000 km between the Moon and the Earth),[^cite_note-112] extending to the Earth–Moon [lagrange points](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagrange_points). This space is called [cislunar space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cislunar_space).[^cite_note-v692-113]

### Magnetic field

The Moon has [an external magnetic field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetic_field_of_the_Moon) of less than 0.2 [nanoteslas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tesla_\(unit\)),[^cite_note-Mighani2020-114] or less than one hundred thousandth [that of Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth's_magnetic_field). The Moon does not have a global [dipolar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dipolar) magnetic field;[^cite_note-115] it only has crustal magnetization.[^cite_note-116] The magnetic field history of the Moon is controversial,[^cite_note-117] and there is still no consensus on the past intensity and timing of the Moons paleomagnetic field.[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-\:0-119] Many paleomagnetic measurements have been taken from lunar samples collected during the Apollo missions. These measurements indicate that the Moon's paleomagnetic field experienced two epochs with distinct characteristics.[^cite_note-Mighani2020-114] In the first epoch (4.5 billion to 3.56 billion years ago), magnetic field intensities reached around 100 microteslas (1 Gauss), close to that of Earth's today.[^cite_note-120] The second epoch was much longer (3.58 billion to 1 billion years ago), yet much weaker, with strengths only reaching 5-7 microteslas.[^cite_note-\:0-119][^cite_note-\:3-121] It is unlikely that a single convection mechanism, thermal or compositional, generated this specific dynamo history without external influence.[^cite_note-\:0-119][^cite_note-122] Theoretically, some of the remnant magnetization may originate from transient magnetic fields generated during large impacts through the expansion of plasma clouds. These clouds are generated during large impacts in an ambient magnetic field. This is supported by the location of the largest crustal magnetizations situated near the [antipodes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antipodes_on_Earth) of the giant impact basins.[^cite_note-123]

Additionally the Moon moves \~27% of the time,[^cite_note-z266-124] or 5–6 days per lunar month in Earth's [magnetotail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetotail), replacing [solar wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_wind) with [Earth wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_wind?action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-u789-125]

### Atmosphere

Main article: [Atmosphere of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmosphere_of_the_Moon)



[Surveyor 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surveyor_7) observes levitating dust, a phenomenon named [Lunar horizon glow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_horizon_glow) can be seen



Observations and modeling of the [Moon's sodium exosphere, tail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_tail_of_the_Moon) and "spot".

The Moon has an [atmosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmosphere) consisting of only an [exosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exosphere),[^cite_note-l546-126] which is so tenuous as to be nearly [vacuum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vacuum), with a total mass of less than 10 tonnes (9.8 long tons; 11 short tons).[^cite_note-127] The surface pressure of this small mass is around 3 × 10<sup>−15</sup> [atm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmosphere_\(unit\)) (0.3 [nPa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanopascal)); it varies with the lunar day. Its sources include [outgassing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outgassing) and [sputtering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputtering), a product of the bombardment of lunar soil by solar wind ions.[^cite_note-L06-20][^cite_note-128] Elements that have been detected include [sodium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium) and [potassium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium), produced by sputtering (also found in the atmospheres of Mercury and [Io](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Io_\(moon\))); [helium-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helium-4) and [neon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neon)[^cite_note-NASA-20150817-129] from the solar wind; and [argon-40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argon), [radon-222](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radon), and [polonium-210](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polonium-210), outgassed after their creation by [radioactive decay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioactive_decay) within the crust and mantle.[^cite_note-Stern1999-130][^cite_note-131] The absence of such neutral species (atoms or molecules) as [oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen), [nitrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitrogen), [carbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon), [hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen) and [magnesium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnesium), which are present in the [regolith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regolith), is not understood.[^cite_note-Stern1999-130] Water vapor has been detected by *[Chandrayaan-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandrayaan-1)* and found to vary with latitude, with a maximum at \~60–70 degrees; it is possibly generated from the [sublimation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sublimation_\(chemistry\)) of water ice in the regolith.[^cite_note-Sridharan2010-132] These gases either return into the regolith because of the Moon's gravity or are lost to space, either through solar radiation pressure or, if they are ionized, by being swept away by the solar wind's magnetic field.[^cite_note-Stern1999-130]

A permanent [Moon dust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_dust) cloud exists around the Moon, generated by small particles from comets. 5 tons of comet particles are estimated to strike the Moon's surface every 24 hours, resulting in the ejection of dust particles.[^cite_note-geo-133] The dust stays above the Moon for approximately 10 minutes, taking 5 minutes to rise, and 5 minutes to fall.[^cite_note-geo-133] On average, 120 kilograms of dust are present above the Moon, rising up to 100 kilometers above the surface.[^cite_note-geo-133] Dust counts made by [LADEE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LADEE)'s Lunar Dust EXperiment (LDEX) found particle counts peaked during the [Geminid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geminid), [Quadrantid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadrantid), [Northern Taurid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taurids), and [Omicron Centaurid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omicron_Centaurids) [meteor showers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meteor_shower), when the Earth, and Moon pass through comet debris. The lunar dust cloud is asymmetric, being denser near the boundary between the Moon's dayside and nightside.[^cite_note-134]

Studies of Moon magma samples retrieved by the [Apollo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_program) missions demonstrate that the Moon had once possessed a relatively thick atmosphere for a period of 70 million years between 3 and 4 billion years ago. This atmosphere, sourced from gases ejected from lunar volcanic eruptions, was twice the thickness of that of present-day [Mars](14640471.md). The ancient lunar atmosphere was eventually stripped away by solar winds and dissipated into space.[^cite_note-John_2017-74]

### Surface conditions



Astronaut [Charles Duke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Duke) working on the Moon with his suit covered in [lunar dust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_dust). Lunar dust is highly abrasive and can cause damage to human lungs and nervous and cardiovascular systems.

[^cite_note-135]

[Ionizing radiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionizing_radiation) from [cosmic rays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmic_ray), their resulting [neutron radiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutron_radiation),[^cite_note-Science_Mission_Directorate_2005-136] and the Sun results in an average radiation level of 1.369 [millisieverts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millisievert) per day during lunar [daytime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daytime),[^cite_note-surface-radiation-16] which is about 2.6 times more than the level on the [International Space Station](15043.md), 5–10 times more than the level during a trans-Atlantic flight, and 200 times more than the level on Earth's surface.[^cite_note-ScienceAlert_2020-137] For further comparison, radiation levels average about 1.84 millisieverts per day on a [flight to Mars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_mission_to_Mars) and about 0.64 millisieverts per day on Mars itself, with some locations on Mars possibly having levels as low as 0.342 millisieverts per day.[^cite_note-Paris_Davies_Tognetti_Zahniser_2020-138][^cite_note-Wall_2013-139] Solar radiation also [electrically charges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Static_electricity) the highly abrasive [lunar dust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_dust) and makes it levitate. This effect contributes to the easy spread of the sticky, lung- and gear-damaging lunar dust.[^cite_note-e657-140]

The lunar surface is exposed to temperature differences ranging from 120 °C to −171 °C depending on the [solar irradiance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_irradiance). Because of the lack of atmosphere, temperatures of different areas vary particularly upon whether they are in sunlight or shadow,[^cite_note-141] making topographical details play a decisive role on local [surface temperatures](37719142.md).[^cite_note-bbc-142] Parts of many craters, particularly the bottoms of many polar craters,[^cite_note-M03-143] are permanently shadowed. These [craters of eternal darkness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crater_of_eternal_darkness) have extremely low temperatures. The *Lunar Reconnaissance Orbiter* measured the lowest summer temperatures in craters at the southern pole at 35 K (−238 °C; −397 °F)[^cite_note-144] and just 26 K (−247 °C; −413 °F) close to the lunar winter solstice in the north polar crater [Hermite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermite_\(crater\)). This is the coldest temperature in the Solar System ever measured by a spacecraft, colder even than the surface of [Pluto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluto).[^cite_note-bbc-142]

Blanketed on top of the Moon's crust is a highly [comminuted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comminution) (broken into ever smaller particles) and [impact gardened](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_gardening) mostly gray surface layer called [regolith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regolith), formed by impact processes. The finer regolith, the [lunar soil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_soil) of [silicon dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_dioxide) glass, has a texture resembling snow and a scent resembling spent [gunpowder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunpowder).[^cite_note-145] The regolith of older surfaces is generally thicker than for younger surfaces: it varies in thickness from 10–15 m (33–49 ft) in the highlands and 4–5 m (13–16 ft) in the maria.[^cite_note-146] Beneath the finely comminuted regolith layer is the megaregolith, a layer of highly fractured bedrock many kilometers thick.[^cite_note-147]

These extreme conditions are considered to make it unlikely for spacecraft to harbor bacterial spores at the Moon for longer than just one lunar orbit.[^cite_note-148]

### Surface features

Main articles: [Selenography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selenography), [Lunar terrane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_terrane), [List of lunar features](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_lunar_features), and [List of quadrangles on the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_quadrangles_on_the_Moon)



[Near side of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_side_of_the_Moon)



[Far side of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon)



East side



West side



Map of the Moon colored by the thickness of the crust, with main regions marked.

The [KREEP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KREEP) rich magma terrane (hatched), with its most prominent part labeled on the near side (left), the [Mare Procellarum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Procellarum) KREEP Terrane (PKT).

The [South Pole–Aitken Terrane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Pole%E2%80%93Aitken_basin) (SPAT) is marked on the far side (right, largest circle).

The 24 crater basins with crustal thinning greater than 200 km (120 mi) are marked (black circles) across both hemispheres.

The rest is the high-thickness [Felspathic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felspathic) Highlands Terrane (FHT; red and white).

[^cite_note-149]

The [topography of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topography_of_the_Moon) has been measured with [laser altimetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laser_altimetry) and [stereo image analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stereoscopy).[^cite_note-150] Its most extensive [topographic feature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_features_on_the_Moon) is the giant far-side [South Pole–Aitken basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Pole%E2%80%93Aitken_basin), some 2,240 km (1,390 mi) in diameter, the largest crater on the Moon and the second-largest confirmed impact [crater in the Solar System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_craters_in_the_Solar_System).[^cite_note-Spudis1994-151][^cite_note-152] At 13 km (8.1 mi) deep, its floor is the lowest point on the surface of the Moon,[^cite_note-Spudis1994-151][^cite_note-153] reaching −9.178 kilometers (−5.703 mi) at [70°22′05″S 172°24′47″W  /  70.368°S 172.413°W  / -70.368; -172.413](https://geohack.toolforge.org/geohack.php?pagename=Moon\&params=70.368_S_172.413_W_globe\:moon_type\:landmark) in a crater within [Antoniadi crater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoniadi_\(lunar_crater\)).[^cite_note-n402-154] The [highest elevations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_mountains_in_the_Solar_System) of the Moon's surface, with the so-called [Selenean summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selenean_summit) at 10.629 kilometers (6.605 mi), are located directly to the northeast ([5°26′28″N 158°39′22″W  /  5.441°N 158.656°W  / 5.441; -158.656](https://geohack.toolforge.org/geohack.php?pagename=Moon\&params=5.441_N_158.656_W_globe\:moon_type\:landmark)),[^cite_note-n402-154] which might have been thickened by the oblique formation impact of the South Pole–Aitken basin.[^cite_note-155] Other large impact basins such as [Imbrium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Imbrium), [Serenitatis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Serenitatis), [Crisium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Crisium), [Smythii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Smythii), and [Orientale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Orientale) possess regionally low elevations and elevated rims.[^cite_note-Spudis1994-151] The far side of the lunar surface is on average about 1.9 km (1.2 mi) higher than that of the near side.[^cite_note-W06-1]

The discovery of [fault scarp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fault_scarp) cliffs suggest that the Moon has shrunk by about 90 meters (300 ft) within the past billion years.[^cite_note-156] Similar shrinkage features exist on [Mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_Mercury). Mare Frigoris, a basin near the north pole long assumed to be geologically dead, has cracked and shifted. Since the Moon does not have tectonic plates, its tectonic activity is slow, and cracks develop as it loses heat.[^cite_note-157]

Scientists have confirmed the presence of a cave on the Moon near the [Sea of Tranquility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_of_Tranquility), not far from the 1969 [Apollo 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_11) landing site.[^cite_note-base-158] The cave, identified as an entry point to a collapsed [lava tube](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_lava_tube), is roughly 45 meters wide and up to 80 m long.[^cite_note-base-158] This discovery marks the first confirmed entry point to a lunar cave. The analysis was based on photos taken in 2010 by NASA's [Lunar Reconnaissance Orbiter](15970718.md).[^cite_note-base-158] The cave's stable temperature of around 17 °C could provide a hospitable environment for future astronauts, protecting them from extreme temperatures, solar radiation, and micrometeorites.[^cite_note-base-158] However, challenges include accessibility and risks of avalanches and cave-ins. This discovery offers potential for future lunar bases or emergency shelters.[^cite_note-base-158]

#### Volcanic features

Main article: [Volcanism on the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanism_on_the_Moon)



Map of the near side of the Moon, with the dark [lunar maria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_mare), the most prominent volcanic features of the Moon, labeled in blue (brown labels are prominent [lunar craters](1621514.md))

The main features visible from Earth by the naked eye are dark and relatively featureless lunar plains called *[maria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_mare)* (sing. *mare*; from Latin meaning 'seas', as they were once believed to be filled with water)[^cite_note-159] which are vast solidified pools of ancient [basaltic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basalt) lava. Although similar to terrestrial basalts, lunar basalts have more iron and no minerals altered by water.[^cite_note-160] The majority of these lava deposits erupted or flowed into the depressions associated with [impact basins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_basins), though the Moon's largest expanse of basalt flooding, [Oceanus Procellarum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanus_Procellarum), does not correspond to an obvious impact basin. Different episodes of lava flow in maria can often be recognized by variations in surface albedo and distinct flow margins.[^cite_note-161]



Old [hardened](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basalt) lava flows of [Mare Imbrium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Imbrium) forming [wrinkle ridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrinkle_ridge).

As the maria formed, cooling and contraction of the basaltic lava created [wrinkle ridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrinkle_ridge) in some areas.[^cite_note-162] These low, sinuous ridges can extend for hundreds of kilometers and often outline buried structures within the mare. Another result of maria formation is the creation of concentric depressions along the edges, known as [arcuate rilles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rille). These features occur as the mare basalts sink inward under their own weight, causing the edges to fracture and separate.[^cite_note-163]

In addition to the visible maria, the Moon has mare deposits covered by ejecta from impacts. Called cryptomares, these hidden mares are likely older than the exposed ones.[^cite_note-164] Conversely, mare lava has obscured many [impact melt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_melt) sheets and pools. Impact melts are formed when intense shock pressures from collisions vaporize and melt zones around the impact site. Where still exposed, impact melt can be distinguished from mare lava by its distribution, albedo, and texture.[^cite_note-165]

[Sinuous rilles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinuous_rilles), found in and around maria, are likely extinct [lava channels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lava_channels) or collapsed [lava tubes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lava_tubes).[^cite_note-166] They typically originate from volcanic [vents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanism_on_the_Moon), meandering and sometimes branching as they progress.[^cite_note-167] The largest examples, such as [Schroter's Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schroter's_Valley) and [Rima Hadley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadley%E2%80%93Apennine), are significantly longer, wider, and deeper than terrestrial lava channels, sometimes featuring bends and sharp turns that again, are uncommon on Earth.[^cite_note-168]



&#x20;[Sinuous rilles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinuous_rilles) features and [lunar lava tube](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_lava_tube) cave *Marius Hills pit* as observed under different solar illumination conditions.

Mare volcanism has altered impact craters in various ways, including filling them to varying degrees, and raising and fracturing their floors from uplift of mare material beneath their interiors. Examples of such craters include [Taruntius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taruntius_\(crater\)) and [Gassendi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gassendi_\(crater\)).[^cite_note-169] Some craters, such as [Hyginus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyginus_\(crater\)), are of wholly volcanic origin, forming as [calderas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caldera) or [collapse pits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pit_crater).[^cite_note-170] Such craters are relatively rare and tend to be smaller (typically a few kilometers wide), shallower, and more irregularly shaped than impact craters. They also lack the upturned rims characteristic of impact craters.[^cite_note-171]

Several [geologic provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geologic_province) containing [shield volcanoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shield_volcano) and volcanic [domes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_dome) are found within the near side maria.[^cite_note-172] There are also some regions of [pyroclastic deposits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyroclastic_rock), [scoria cones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scoria_cones) and [non-basaltic domes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanism_on_the_Moon) made of particularly high-viscosity lava.[^cite_note-173]

Almost all maria are on the near side of the Moon, and cover 31% of the surface of the near side[^cite_note-worldbook-90] compared with 2% of the far side.[^cite_note-174] This is likely due to a [concentration of heat-producing elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KREEP) under the crust on the near side, which would have caused the underlying mantle to heat up, partially melt, rise to the surface and erupt.[^cite_note-S06-104][^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176] Most of the Moon's [mare basalts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_mare) erupted during the [Imbrian period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imbrian), 3.3–3.7 billion years ago, though some are as young as 1.2 billion years[^cite_note-Hiesinger-75] and as old as 4.2 billion years.[^cite_note-Papike-76]

In 2006, a study of [Ina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ina_\(crater\)), a tiny depression in [Lacus Felicitatis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lacus_Felicitatis), found jagged, relatively dust-free features that, because of the lack of erosion by infalling debris, appeared to be only 2 million years old.[^cite_note-Berardelli-177] [Moonquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonquake) and releases of gas indicate continued lunar activity.[^cite_note-Berardelli-177] Evidence of recent lunar volcanism has been identified at 70 [irregular mare patches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irregular_mare_patch), some less than 50 million years old. This raises the possibility of a much warmer lunar mantle than previously believed, at least on the near side where the deep crust is substantially warmer because of the greater concentration of radioactive elements.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181] Evidence has been found for 2–10 million years old basaltic volcanism within the crater Lowell,[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183] inside the Orientale basin. Some combination of an initially hotter mantle and local enrichment of heat-producing elements in the mantle could be responsible for prolonged activities on the far side in the Orientale basin.[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185]

The lighter-colored regions of the Moon are called *terrae*, or more commonly *highlands*, because they are higher than most maria. They have been radiometrically dated to having formed 4.4 billion years ago and may represent [plagioclase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plagioclase) [cumulates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumulates) of the lunar magma ocean.[^cite_note-Hiesinger-75][^cite_note-Papike-76] In contrast to Earth, no major lunar mountains are believed to have formed as a result of tectonic events.[^cite_note-186]

The concentration of maria on the near side likely reflects the substantially thicker crust of the highlands of the Far Side, which may have formed in a slow-velocity impact of a second moon of Earth a few tens of millions of years after the Moon's formation.[^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188] Alternatively, it may be a consequence of asymmetrical [tidal heating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_heating) when the Moon was much closer to the Earth.[^cite_note-189]

#### Impact craters

Further information: [List of craters on the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_craters_on_the_Moon)



The heavily cratered surface of the Moon, particularly where no mare formed, is discernable when viewed close up, with crater ridges highlighted at the [terminator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terminator_\(solar\)) and the two most prominent craters on the near side, [Tycho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tycho_\(lunar_crater\)) (left) and [Copernicus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copernicus_\(lunar_crater\)) (top) visible, featuring long bright impact-streaks.

A gray, 9feature is a circular ringed structure with high walled sides and a lower central peak: the entire surface out to the horizon is filled with similar structures that are smaller and overlapping.

A view of a larger crater, the three-kilometer-deep [Daedalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daedalus_\(crater\)) on the [Moon's far side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon)



[Apollo 17](1971.md) astronaut [Harrison H. Schmitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrison_H._Schmitt) next to the large Moon boulder nicknamed "[Tracy's Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tracy's_Rock)" in the [Taurus–Littrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taurus%E2%80%93Littrow) of the [Mare Serenitatis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Serenitatis) on the near side of the Moon

A major geologic process that has affected the Moon's surface is [impact cratering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_crater),[^cite_note-190] with craters formed when asteroids and comets collide with the lunar surface. There are estimated to be roughly 300,000 craters wider than 1 km (0.6 mi) on the Moon's near side.[^cite_note-191] Lunar craters exhibit a variety of forms, depending on their size. In order of increasing diameter, the basic types are simple craters with smooth bowl shaped interiors and upturned rims, [complex craters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Complex_crater) with flat floors, terraced walls and central peaks, [peak ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peak_ring) basins, and [multi-ring basins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-ring_basin) with two or more concentric rings of peaks.[^cite_note-192] The vast majority of impact craters are circular, but some, like [Cantor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantor_\(crater\)) and [Janssen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janssen_\(lunar_crater\)),[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194] have more polygonal outlines, possibly guided by underlying tectonic rifts.[^cite_note-195] Others, such as the [Messier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messier_\(crater\)) pair, [Schiller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schiller_\(crater\)), and [Daniell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniell_\(crater\)), are elongated. Such elongation can result from highly oblique impacts, [binary asteroid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binary_asteroid) impacts, fragmentation of impactors before surface strike, or closely spaced [secondary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_crater) impacts.[^cite_note-196]

#### Lunar swirls

Main article: [Lunar swirls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_swirls)



Wide-angle image of a lunar swirl, the 70-kilometer-long [Reiner Gamma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reiner_Gamma)

Lunar swirls are enigmatic features found across the Moon's surface. They are characterized by a high albedo, appear optically immature (i.e. have the optical characteristics of a relatively young [regolith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regolith)),[^cite_note-197] and often have a sinuous shape. Their shape is often accentuated by low [albedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albedo) regions that wind between the bright swirls. They are located in places with enhanced surface [magnetic fields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetic_field) and many are located at the [antipodal point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antipodal_point) of major impacts. Well known swirls include the [Reiner Gamma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reiner_Gamma) feature and [Mare Ingenii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Ingenii). They are hypothesized to be areas that have been partially shielded from the [solar wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_wind), resulting in slower [space weathering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_weathering).[^cite_note-198]

### Presence of water

Main article: [Lunar water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_water)



Distribution of surface ice at the Moon's South (left) and North poles (right), as viewed by NASA's [Moon Mineralogy Mapper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Mineralogy_Mapper) (M

3

) spectrometer onboard India's [Chandrayaan-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandrayaan-1) orbiter

Liquid water cannot persist on the lunar surface. When exposed to solar radiation, water quickly decomposes through a process known as [photodissociation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photodissociation) and is lost to space.[^cite_note-199] However, since the 1960s, scientists have hypothesized that water ice may be deposited by impacting [comets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comets) or possibly produced by the reaction of oxygen-rich lunar rocks, and hydrogen from [solar wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_wind), leaving traces of water which could possibly persist in cold, permanently shadowed craters at either pole on the Moon.[^cite_note-Margot1999-200][^cite_note-201] Computer simulations suggest that up to 14,000 km<sup>2</sup> (5,400 sq mi) of the surface may be in permanent shadow.[^cite_note-M03-143] The presence of usable quantities of water on the Moon is an important factor in rendering [lunar habitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonization_of_the_Moon) as a cost-effective plan; the alternative of transporting water from Earth would be prohibitively expensive.[^cite_note-seedhouse2009-202]

In years since, signatures of water have been found to exist on the lunar surface.[^cite_note-moonwater_18032010-203] In 1994, the [bistatic radar experiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clementine_mission#Bistatic_Radar_Experiment) located on the *[Clementine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clementine_\(spacecraft\))* spacecraft, indicated the existence of small, frozen pockets of water close to the surface. However, later radar observations by [Arecibo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arecibo_Telescope), suggest these findings may rather be rocks ejected from young impact craters.[^cite_note-204] In 1998, the [neutron spectrometer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_Prospector#Neutron_Spectrometer_\(NS\)) on the *Lunar Prospector* spacecraft showed that high concentrations of hydrogen are present in the first meter of depth in the regolith near the polar regions.[^cite_note-Feldman1998-205] Volcanic lava beads, brought back to Earth aboard Apollo 15, showed small amounts of water in their interior.[^cite_note-Saal2008-206]

The 2008 *[Chandrayaan-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandrayaan-1)* spacecraft has since confirmed the existence of surface water ice, using the on-board [Moon Mineralogy Mapper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Mineralogy_Mapper). The spectrometer observed absorption lines common to [hydroxyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroxyl), in reflected sunlight, providing evidence of large quantities of water ice, on the lunar surface. The spacecraft showed that concentrations may possibly be as high as 1,000 [ppm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parts_per_million).[^cite_note-Pieters2009-207] Using the mapper's reflectance spectra, indirect lighting of areas in shadow confirmed water ice within 20° latitude of both poles in 2018.[^cite_note-208] In 2009, *[LCROSS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LCROSS)* sent a 2,300 kg (5,100 lb) impactor into a [permanently shadowed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanently_shadowed_crater) polar crater, and detected at least 100 kg (220 lb) of water in a plume of ejected material.[^cite_note-Planetary-209][^cite_note-Colaprete-210] Another examination of the LCROSS data showed the amount of detected water to be closer to 155 ± 12 kg (342 ± 26 lb).[^cite_note-Colaprete2010-211]

In May 2011, 615–1410 ppm water in [melt inclusions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melt_inclusions) in lunar sample 74220 was reported,[^cite_note-hauri-212] the famous high-titanium "orange glass soil" of volcanic origin collected during the [Apollo 17](1971.md) mission in 1972.[^cite_note-hauri-212] The inclusions were formed during explosive eruptions on the Moon approximately 3.7 billion years ago.[^cite_note-hauri-212] This concentration is comparable with that of magma in Earth's [upper mantle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_mantle).[^cite_note-hauri-212]

Analysis of the findings of the Moon Mineralogy Mapper (M3) revealed in August 2018 for the first time "definitive evidence" for water-ice on the lunar surface.[^cite_note-\:1-213][^cite_note-214] The data revealed the distinct reflective signatures of water-ice, as opposed to dust and other reflective substances.[^cite_note-\:2-215] The ice deposits were found on the North and South poles, although it is more abundant in the South, where water is trapped in permanently shadowed craters and crevices, allowing it to persist as ice on the surface since they are shielded from the sun.[^cite_note-\:1-213][^cite_note-\:2-215]

In October 2020, astronomers reported detecting [molecular water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water) on the sunlit surface of the Moon by several independent spacecraft, including the [Stratospheric Observatory for Infrared Astronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stratospheric_Observatory_for_Infrared_Astronomy) (SOFIA).[^cite_note-NA-20201026-216][^cite_note-NA-20201026poh-217][^cite_note-WP-20201026-218][^cite_note-NYT-20201026-219]

## &#x20;Earth–Moon system

See also: [Satellite system (astronomy)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_system_\(astronomy\)) and [Claimed moons of Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claimed_moons_of_Earth)

### Orbit

Main articles: [Orbit of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbit_of_the_Moon) and [Lunar theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_theory)



A [Sun-synchronous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun-synchronous_orbit) view of the day-side of Earth from the Sun–Earth [Lagrange point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagrange_point) L1, with Earth rotating and the Moon passing on its orbit in between the observing [DSCOVR satellite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DSCOVR_satellite) and Earth, showing [the Moon's far side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon) when it is illuminated and the Moon's day-side

The [semi-major axis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-major_axis) of the geocentric lunar orbit, called the [lunar distance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_distance), is approximately 385,000 km (239,000 mi), or 1.3 [light-seconds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light-second), comparable to going [around Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumference_of_Earth) approximately 9.6 times.[^cite_note-distance_ratio-220][^cite_note-allen-221] The Moon's orbit is not perfectly circular but rather slightly elliptical, with an [orbital eccentricity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_eccentricity) of 0.055. This means the distance between the Moon and Earth varies from around 356,400 km (221,500 mi) ([perigee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perigee)) to 406,700 km (252,700 mi) (apogee), making the Moon's distance and apparent size fluctuate up to 14%.[^cite_note-size1-222][^cite_note-size2-223]

The Earth–Moon system has a shared center of mass, or [barycenter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barycenter), which is located 5,000 km (3,100 mi) (about 3/4 of Earth's radius) from the Earth's center. Both the Earth and the Moon actually orbit this center of mass, so Earth wobbles around this point for every orbit the Moon makes. This barycenter is slowly moving outward as tidal friction drains energy from the rotating pair, and eventually the barycenter will be outside the Earth.[^cite_note-els-45]<sup>: 69 </sup>

The Moon makes a complete orbit around Earth with respect to the fixed stars, its [sidereal period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidereal_period), about once every 27.3 days.[^cite_note-orbpd-224] However, because the Earth–Moon system moves at the same time in its orbit around the Sun, it takes slightly longer to return to the same [lunar phase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_phase),[^cite_note-225] 29.5 days,[^cite_note-synpd-226][^cite_note-worldbook-90] completing a full cycle as seen from Earth. This [synodic period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synodic_period) or synodic month is commonly known as the [lunar month](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_month) and is equal to the length of the [solar day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_day) on the Moon.[^cite_note-Day-227]

The Moon's orbital plane maintains a relatively constant inclination, about 5 degrees, relative to Earth's orbit around the Sun (the [ecliptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecliptic)).[^cite_note-Lang2011-3] The direction of this tilt gradually rotates westward, called [nodal precession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_precession#Nodal_precession), completing a circle once every 18.61 years.[^cite_note-tilt-228] This means that the Moon's orbit changes its tilt relative to Earth's equator from 18 to 28 degrees in that time.[^cite_note-tilt-228] The shape of the orbit rotates as well, along the plane of the Moon's orbit, called [apsidal precession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_precession#Apsidal_precession).[^cite_note-apsis-229] Measured from the [intersection points](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_node) of the Moon's orbit with the ecliptic plane (which themselves move westward by nodal precession), it rotates eastward with a period of 8.85 years.[^cite_note-apsis-229] Unlike most [regular satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regular_satellite) of other planets, the Moon's orbital plane is closer to the [ecliptic plane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecliptic_plane) than to their planet's [equatorial plane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equatorial_plane). The Moon's orbit is subtly [perturbed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perturbation_\(astronomy\)) by the Sun and Earth in many small, complex and interacting ways. These follow-on effects are mathematically described by [Cassini's laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassini's_laws).[^cite_note-Beletskii2-230]



Minimum, mean and maximum distances of the Moon from Earth with its angular diameter as seen from Earth's surface to scale

### Rotation



Comparison between the Moon on the left, rotating tidally locked (correct), and with the Moon on the right, without rotation (incorrect)

Due to it being [tidally locked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidally_locked) to Earth, the Moon has a 1\:1 spin–orbit resonance. The rotation–orbit ratio means the Moon's orbital period around Earth is equal to its [rotation period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotation_period), called synchronous rotation. This results in it always keeping nearly the same face turned towards the Earth as it orbits.[^cite_note-231] The side of the Moon that faces Earth is called the [near side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_side), and the opposite the [far side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon). Since the Moon is not tidally locked to the Sun, lunar daylight and night times both occur around the Moon. The far side is often inaccurately called the "dark side", but it is in fact illuminated as often as the near side: once every 29.5 Earth days. During [dark moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dark_moon) to [new moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_moon), the near side is dark.[^cite_note-232]

Lunar night is the darkest on the far side and [during lunar eclipses on the near side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_eclipses_on_the_Moon) (and darker than a moonless night on Earth). During its night, the near side is illuminated by [Earthlight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthlight) to the extent that lunar surface features may be observable from Earth. Earthshine makes the night on the near side about 43 times brighter, and sometimes even 55 times brighter than a night on Earth illuminated by the light of the full moon.[^cite_note-u988-233]

The moon's [axial tilt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axial_tilt) with respect to the [ecliptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecliptic) is 1.5427°,[^cite_note-234] so in each [draconic year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Draconic_year) (346.62 days), the sun moves from being 1.5427° north of the lunar equator to being 1.5427° south of it and then back. This is analogous to how, on Earth, the sun's position moves from the [Tropic of Cancer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropic_of_Cancer) to the [Tropic of Capricorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropic_of_Capricorn) and back every [tropical year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_year). This puts the poles of the moon in darkness (or with only part of the sun visible) for half a draconic year and in sunlight for half a draconic year.[^cite_note-235] The amount of sunlight falling on horizontal areas near the poles depends on the [altitude angle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altitude_angle) of the sun. These seasons have little effect in more equatorial areas.[^cite_note-236]

The Moon originally rotated at a faster rate, but early in its history its rotation slowed and became tidally locked in this orientation as a result of [frictional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friction) effects associated with [tidal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_force) deformations caused by Earth.[^cite_note-237] With time, the energy of rotation of the Moon on its axis was dissipated as heat, until there was no net rotation of the Moon relative to Earth. In 2016, planetary scientists using data collected on the 1998–99 NASA *[Lunar Prospector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_Prospector)* mission found two hydrogen-rich areas (most likely former water ice) on opposite sides of the Moon. It is speculated that these patches were the poles of the Moon billions of years ago before it was tidally locked to Earth.[^cite_note-238]

### Tidal effects

Further information: [Tide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tide), [Tidal force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_force), and [Theory of tides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory_of_tides)



Simplified diagram of Earth bulging, being pulled and stretched toward the Moon by its gravity, which is the main driver of the [tides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tide). The Ocean and Earth are being pulled more where it is closer to the Moon, causing [tidal forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_force) to be weaker at the far-side of Earth creating a second bulge and high-tide. The animation shows the change of the Moon's position on its inclined orbit.

The gravitational attraction that Earth and the Moon (as well as the Sun) exert on each other manifests in a slightly greater attraction on the sides closest to each other, resulting in [tidal forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_forces).[^cite_note-Tyson-239] [Ocean tides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ocean_tides) are the most widely experienced result of this, but tidal forces also considerably affect other mechanics of Earth, as well as the Moon and their system.[^cite_note-240][^cite_note-241]

The lunar solid crust experiences tides of around 10 cm (4 in) amplitude over 27 days, with three components: a fixed one due to Earth, because they are in [synchronous rotation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synchronous_rotation), a variable tide due to orbital eccentricity and inclination, and a small varying component from the Sun.[^cite_note-touma1994-242] The Earth-induced variable component arises from changing distance and [libration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libration), a result of the Moon's orbital eccentricity and inclination (if the Moon's orbit were perfectly circular and un-inclined, there would only be solar tides).[^cite_note-touma1994-242] According to recent research, scientists suggest that the Moon's influence on the Earth may contribute to maintaining [Earth's magnetic field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth's_magnetic_field).[^cite_note-243]

The cumulative effects of stress built up by these tidal forces produces [moonquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonquakes). Moonquakes are much less common and weaker than are earthquakes, although moonquakes can last for up to an hour – significantly longer than terrestrial quakes – because of scattering of the seismic vibrations in the dry fragmented upper crust.[^cite_note-quake-244] The existence of moonquakes was an unexpected discovery from [seismometers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seismometer) placed on the Moon by [Apollo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_program) [astronauts](664.md) from 1969 through 1972.[^cite_note-quake-244]

The most commonly known effect of tidal forces is elevated sea levels called ocean tides.[^cite_note-Lambeck1977-245] While the Moon exerts most of the tidal forces, the Sun also exerts tidal forces and therefore contributes to the tides as much as 40% of the Moon's tidal force; producing in interplay the [spring and neap tides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spring_tide).[^cite_note-Lambeck1977-245]

The tides are two bulges in the Earth's oceans, one on the side facing the Moon and the other on the side opposite. As the Earth rotates on its axis, one of the ocean bulges (high tide) is held in place "under" the Moon, while another such tide is opposite. The tide under the Moon is explained by the Moon's gravity being stronger on the water close to it. The tide on the opposite side can be explained either by the centrifugal force as the Earth orbits the [barycenter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barycenter) or by the water's inertia as the Moon's gravity is stronger on the solid Earth close to it and it is pulled away from the farther water.[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247]
