Thus, there are two high tides, and two low tides in about 24 hours.[^cite_note-Lambeck1977-245] Since the Moon is orbiting the Earth in the same direction of the Earth's rotation, the high tides occur about every 12 hours and 25 minutes; the 25 minutes is due to the Moon's time to orbit the Earth.[^cite_note-248]

If the Earth were a water world (one with no continents) it would produce a tide of only one meter, and that tide would be very predictable, but the ocean tides are greatly modified by other effects:

- the frictional coupling of water to Earth's rotation through the ocean floors

- the [inertia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inertia) of water's movement

- ocean basins that grow shallower near land

- the sloshing of water between different ocean basins

  [^cite_note-249]

### System evolution

Further information: [Tidal acceleration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_acceleration)

Delays in the tidal peaks of both ocean and solid-body tides cause [torque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torque) in opposition to the Earth's rotation. This "drains" [angular momentum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angular_momentum) and rotational [kinetic energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinetic_energy) from Earth's rotation, slowing the Earth's rotation.[^cite_note-Lambeck1977-245][^cite_note-touma1994-242] 3.2 billion years ago, during the [Archean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archean), the solar day was only around 13 hours. The angular momentum, lost from the rotation of the Earth, is transferred to the Moon in a process known as [tidal acceleration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_acceleration), which lifts the Moon into a higher orbit while lowering orbital speed around the Earth.[^cite_note-m870-250]

Thus the distance between Earth and Moon is increasing, and the Earth's rotation is slowing in reaction.[^cite_note-touma1994-242] Measurements from laser reflectors left during the Apollo missions ([lunar ranging experiments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_Laser_Ranging_experiments)) have found that the Moon's distance increases by 38 mm (1.5 in) per year (roughly the rate at which human fingernails grow).[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252][^cite_note-253] [Atomic clocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_clock) show that Earth's Day lengthens by about 17 [microseconds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microsecond) every year,[^cite_note-254][^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256] slowly increasing the rate at which [UTC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UTC) is adjusted by [leap seconds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leap_second).

Over time this tidal drag causes the Earth's rotation and the Moon's orbital period to match, resulting in the lighter body becoming tidally locked (as the Moon currently is). Theoretically, in 50 billion years,[^cite_note-Universe_Today_2016-257] the Earth's rotation will slow to match the Moon's orbital period, causing the Earth to always present the same side to the Moon. Long before this would happen, however, the Sun will become a [red giant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_giant#The_Sun_as_a_red_giant), possibly engulfing the Earth and the Moon.[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259] If the Earth–Moon system survives this engulfment, as a 2026 study suggests,[^cite_note-Esseldeurs_et_al_2026-260] the drag from the solar atmosphere could cause the orbit of the Moon to decay. Once the orbit of the Moon closes to a distance of 18,470 km (11,480 mi), it will cross Earth's [Roche limit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roche_limit) and the tidal interaction with Earth would break the Moon apart. It would turn into a [ring system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ring_system), with most of the orbiting rings decaying and falling to Earth.[^cite_note-f170-88]

## &#x20;Observation

Main article: [Lunar observation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_observation)

### Apparent size and position

The Moon appears in Earth's sky differently depending on the position of observation on Earth and the time of [lunar year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_year), [lunar month](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_month) and day on Earth. During a year the Moon [culminates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culmination) during a day at different [altitudes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altitude_\(astronomy\)).[^cite_note-261] The full Moon appears highest in the sky during winter and lowest during summer, unlike the Sun,[^cite_note-262] which is true for the seasons of both of Earth's northern and southern hemispheres.

At the [North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Pole) and [South Poles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Pole) the Moon is 24 hours above the horizon for two weeks every [tropical month](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_month) (about 27.3 days), comparable to the [polar day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polar_day) of the [tropical year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_year). [Zooplankton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zooplankton) in the [Arctic](36971.md) use [moonlight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonlight) when the Sun is [below the horizon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polar_night) for months on end.[^cite_note-263]

On average the Moon's [angular diameter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angular_diameter) is about 0.52°, roughly the same apparent size as the Sun (see [§ Eclipses](#Eclipses)). Its apparent size and brightness vary due to its elliptical orbit, appearing up to 30% brighter and 14% larger at perigee compared to apogee. In addition, a purely psychological effect, known as the [Moon illusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_illusion), makes the Moon appear larger when close to the [horizon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horizon).[^cite_note-264]

### Orientation and appearance

Over one lunar month more than half of the Moon's surface can be seen from Earth's surface.

[Libration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libration), the slight variation in the Moon's [apparent size](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apparent_size) and viewing angle over a single lunar month as viewed from somewhere on the Earth's Northern Hemisphere.

The apparent orientation of the Moon depends on its position in the sky and the hemisphere of the Earth from which it is being viewed. In the [Northern Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Hemisphere) it appears upside down compared to the view from the [Southern Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Hemisphere).[^cite_note-265] Sometimes the "horns" of a crescent moon appear to be pointing more upwards than sideways. This phenomenon is called a [wet moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_moon) and occurs more frequently in the [tropics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropics).[^cite_note-266]

The tidally locked [synchronous rotation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synchronous_rotation) of the Moon as it orbits the Earth results in only one side of the Moon, its so-called [near side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_side), being visible from Earth. That said, while the movement of the Moon is in resonance, it still is not without nuances such as [libration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libration), resulting in slightly changing perspectives, making over time and location on Earth about 59% of the Moon's surface visible from Earth.[^cite_note-Libration-267] Due to lunar libration, it is sometimes possible to see slightly more than the full moon (up to about 101%) or a small part of its far side (up to 5%).[^cite_note-268]

#### Observational phenomena



[Lunar impact flash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transient_lunar_phenomenon#Impact_flash) image shown superimposed on the Moon

There has been historical controversy over whether observed features on the Moon's surface change over time. Today, many of these claims are thought to be illusory, resulting from observation under different lighting conditions, poor [astronomical seeing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomical_seeing), or inadequate drawings. However, [outgassing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outgassing) does occasionally occur and could be responsible for a minor percentage of the reported [lunar transient phenomena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_transient_phenomena). Recently, it has been suggested that a roughly 3 km (1.9 mi) diameter region of the lunar surface was modified by a gas release event about a million years ago.[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270]

### Phases

Main article: [Lunar phase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_phase)



The monthly changes in the angle between the direction of sunlight and view from Earth, and the [phases of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_phase) that result, as viewed from the [Northern Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Hemisphere). The [Earth–Moon distance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth%E2%80%93Moon_distance) is not to scale.

The Moon rotates, as it orbits Earth, changing orientation toward the Sun, experiencing a [lunar day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_day). A lunar day is equal to one [lunar month](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_month) (one synodic orbit around Earth). The changing position of the illumination of the Moon by the Sun during a lunar day is observable from Earth as the changing [lunar phases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_phase). The phases of the moon follow a repeating cycle, with its starting point at new moon. In the first half of the cycle, the sequence is new moon, crescent, quarter, gibbous, and full moon. In the second half of the cycle, the sequence is reversed, then the cycle repeats.[^cite_note-r040-271]

The brightness and apparent size of the Moon changes also due to its elliptic orbit around Earth. Consequently, given the same phase, the Moon's brightness also varies by up to 30% between apogee and perigee.[^cite_note-272] At [perigee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perigee) (closest), since the Moon is up to 14% closer to Earth than at [apogee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apogee) (most distant), it subtends a [solid angle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solid_angle) which is up to 30% larger. A full (or new) moon at such a position is called a [supermoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supermoon).[^cite_note-size1-222][^cite_note-size2-223][^cite_note-273]

Each of the four intermediate phases of the Moon lasts, on average, about seven days (approximately 7.38 days), but this duration can vary by about ±11% due to the change in the distance between the Moon and Earth between apogee (farthest point) and perigee (closest point).

The number of days since the most recent new moon is called the Moon's age.[^cite_note-274] A complete cycle of lunar phases is known as a lunation.[^cite_note-275]

The approximate age and phase of the Moon for any date can be calculated by determining the number of days since a known new moon (e.g., January 1, 1900, or August 11, 1999) and dividing that value by the average length of a synodic month (29.53059 days).[^cite_note-276] The remainder of the division represents the Moon's age. This method assumes a perfectly circular orbit and does not consider the exact time of the new moon, so results may be off by a few hours. Accuracy decreases as the date moves away from the reference date. This simplified calculation is suitable for many purposes, but more precise applications require more complex calculation.

### True albedo and color of the surface

See also: [Moonlight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonlight) and [Halo (optical phenomenon)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halo_\(optical_phenomenon\))

The Moon's appearance is shaped by its reflective properties and surface composition. Its [albedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albedo) is about 0.12,[^cite_note-277] comparable to worn [asphalt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asphalt_concrete), making it relatively dark despite its brightness in the night sky. This low albedo represents the reflectance of the lunar regolith, a layer of fine rock fragments created by meteorite impacts. Light scattering by the regolith causes the Moon to appear much brighter at full moon than at quarter phases. The Moon also exhibits retro-reflection,[^cite_note-278] scattering light back toward its source and creating a uniform brightness across its disc without significant limb darkening.



The changing apparent color of the Moon, filtered by Earth's atmosphere

The Moon's true color, without atmospheric effects, is a muted brownish-gray.[^cite_note-279] This results from silicate minerals in the regolith, with darker basaltic plains (maria) and lighter feldspar-rich highlands. The maria, formed by ancient volcanic flows, contain more iron and titanium, giving them a darker tone.

From Earth, the Moon's color can be altered by the atmosphere, at times appearing red or blue. It may appear red during a [lunar eclipse](18298.md), because of the red spectrum of the Sun's light being [refracted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refracted) onto the Moon by Earth's atmosphere. Because of this red color, lunar eclipses are also sometimes called blood moons. The Moon can also seem red when it appears at low angles and through a thick atmosphere. The Moon may also appear blue depending on the presence of certain particles in the air,[^cite_note-Science_Mission_Directorate_2020-280] such as volcanic particles.[^cite_note-281] Because the words "blood moon" and "[blue moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_moon)" can also be used to refer to specific [full moons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_moon) of the year, they do not always refer to the presence of red or blue [moonlight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonlight).[^cite_note-282][^cite_note-283]

### Eclipses

Main articles: [Solar eclipse](5024887.md), [Lunar eclipse](18298.md), and [Eclipse cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eclipse_cycle)



A [solar eclipse](5024887.md) causes the Sun to be covered, revealing the white [corona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_corona).



The Moon, tinted reddish, during a [lunar eclipse](18298.md)

Eclipses only occur when the Sun, Earth, and Moon are all in a straight line (termed "[syzygy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syzygy_\(astronomy\))"). [Solar eclipses](5024887.md) occur at [new moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_moon), when the Moon is between the Sun and Earth. In contrast, [lunar eclipses](18298.md) occur at full moon, when Earth is between the Sun and Moon. The apparent size of the Moon is roughly the same as that of the Sun, with both being viewed at close to one-half a degree wide. The Sun is much larger than the Moon, but it is the vastly greater distance that gives it the same apparent size as the much closer and much smaller Moon from the perspective of Earth. The variations in apparent size, due to the non-circular orbits, are nearly the same as well, though occurring in different cycles. This makes possible both [total](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_eclipse) (with the Moon appearing larger than the Sun) and [annular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annular_eclipse) (with the Moon appearing smaller than the Sun) solar eclipses.[^cite_note-284] In a total eclipse, the Moon completely covers the disc of the Sun and the [solar corona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_corona) becomes visible to the [naked eye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naked_eye).[^cite_note-285]

Because the distance between the Moon and Earth is very slowly increasing over time,[^cite_note-Lambeck1977-245] the angular diameter of the Moon is decreasing. As the Sun evolves toward becoming a [red giant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_giant), its size and its apparent diameter in the sky are slowly increasing.[^cite_note-size_changes-286] The combination of these two changes means that hundreds of millions of years ago, the Moon would always completely cover the Sun on solar eclipses, and no annular eclipses were possible. Likewise, hundreds of millions of years in the future, the Moon will no longer cover the Sun completely, and total solar eclipses will not occur.[^cite_note-fourmilab-287]

As the Moon's orbit around Earth is inclined by about 5.145° (5° 9′) to the [orbit of Earth around the Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecliptic), eclipses do not occur at every full and new moon. For an eclipse to occur, the Moon must be near the intersection of the two orbital planes.[^cite_note-eclipse-288] The periodicity and recurrence of eclipses of the Sun by the Moon, and of the Moon by Earth, is described by the [saros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saros_\(astronomy\)), which has a period of approximately 18 years.[^cite_note-289]

Because the Moon continuously blocks the view of a half-degree-wide circular area of the sky,[^cite_note-area-290][^cite_note-291] the related phenomenon of [occultation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occultation) occurs when a bright star or planet passes behind the Moon and is occulted: hidden from view. In this way, a solar eclipse is an occultation of the Sun. Because the Moon is comparatively close to Earth, occultations of individual stars are not visible everywhere on the planet, nor at the same time. Because of the [precession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precession) of the lunar orbit, each year different stars are occulted.[^cite_note-292]

## &#x20;History of scientific understanding and exploration

Main articles: [Exploration of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exploration_of_the_Moon), [List of spacecraft that orbited the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_spacecraft_that_orbited_the_Moon), [List of missions to the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_missions_to_the_Moon), and [List of lunar probes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_lunar_probes)

### Pre-telescopic observation (before 1609)

It is possible that the waxing and waning of the [lunar phases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_phases) were tracked to keep time in the oldest cave paintings of bulls and geometric shapes, from up to 40,000 years ago,[^cite_note-e093-293] or by the use of [tally sticks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tally_stick) from 20–30,000 years ago.[^cite_note-Burton2011-294] Aspects of the Moon were identified and aggregated in [lunar deities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_deities) from prehistoric times and were eventually documented and put into symbols from the very first instances of writing in the 4th millennium BC. One of the earliest-discovered possible depictions of the Moon is a 3,000 BCE rock carving *Orthostat 47* at [Knowth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowth), Ireland.[^cite_note-Knowth-295][^cite_note-spacetoday-296] The crescent depicting the Moon as with the lunar deity [Nanna/Sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sin_\(mythology\)) have been found from the 3rd millennium BC.[^cite_note-BlackGreen1992-297]

The oldest named astronomer and poet [Enheduanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enheduanna), [Akkadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_Empire) high priestess to the lunar deity Nanna/Sin and princess, daughter of [Sargon the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sargon_the_Great) (c. 2334 – c. 2279 BCE), had the Moon tracked in her chambers.[^cite_note-c099-298] The oldest identified depiction of the Moon in an astronomical relation to other astronomical features is the [Nebra sky disc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebra_sky_disc) from c. 1800–1600 BCE, depicting features like the [Pleiades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pleiades) next to the Moon.[^cite_note-g361-299][^cite_note-k874-300]



The Nebra sky disc (c. 1800–1600 BCE), found near a possible [astronomical complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomical_complex), most likely depicting the Sun or full Moon, the Moon as a crescent, the Pleiades and the summer and winter solstices as strips of gold on the side of the disc,

[^cite_note-Meller_2021-301]

[^cite_note-302]

&#x20;with the top representing the horizon

[^cite_note-\:03-303]

&#x20;and [north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North).

The [ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece) philosopher [Anaxagoras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anaxagoras) (d. 428 BC) reasoned that the Sun and Moon were both giant spherical rocks, and that the Moon reflected the Sun's light.[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-Needham1986-305]<sup>: 227 </sup> Elsewhere in the 5th century BC to 4th century BC, [Babylonian astronomers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonian_astronomers) had recorded the 18-year [Saros cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saros_cycle) of [lunar eclipses](18298.md),[^cite_note-306]  and [Indian astronomers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_astronomy) had described the Moon's monthly elongation.[^cite_note-Sarma-Ast-Ind-307] The [Chinese astronomer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_astronomer) [Shi Shen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shi_Shen) ([fl.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fl.) 4th century BC) gave instructions for predicting solar and lunar eclipses.[^cite_note-Needham1986-305]<sup>: 411 </sup>

In [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle)'s (384–322 BC) [description of the universe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Heavens), the Moon marked the boundary between the spheres of the mutable elements (earth, water, air and fire), and the imperishable stars of [aether](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aether_\(classical_element\)), an influential concept from [Aristotelian physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotelian_physics) that would dominate for centuries.[^cite_note-308] [Archimedes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archimedes) (287–212 BC) designed a planetarium that could calculate the motions of the Moon and other objects in the Solar System.[^cite_note-309] In the 2nd century BC, [Seleucus of Seleucia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucus_of_Seleucia) correctly thought that tides were due to the attraction of the Moon, and that their height depends on the Moon's position relative to the Sun.[^cite_note-310] In the same century, [Aristarchus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Sizes_and_Distances_\(Aristarchus\)) computed the size and distance of the Moon from Earth, obtaining a value of about twenty times the radius of Earth for the distance.[^cite_note-311]

The Chinese of the [Han dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_dynasty) believed the Moon to be energy equated to *[qi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qi)* and their 'radiating influence' theory recognized that the light of the Moon was merely a reflection of the Sun; [Jing Fang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jing_Fang) (78–37 BC) noted the sphericity of the Moon.[^cite_note-Needham1986-305]<sup>: 413–414 </sup> [Ptolemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemy) (90–168 AD) greatly improved on the numbers of Aristarchus, calculating a mean distance of 59 times Earth's radius and a diameter of 0.292 Earth diameters, close to the correct values of about 60 and 0.273 respectively.[^cite_note-312] In 510 AD, the Indian astronomer [Aryabhata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aryabhata) mentioned in his *[Aryabhatiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aryabhatiya)* that reflected sunlight is the cause of the shining of the Moon.[^cite_note-Hayashi08Aryabhata-313][^cite_note-314] The astronomer and physicist [Ibn al-Haytham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_al-Haytham) (965–1039) found that sunlight was not reflected from the Moon like a mirror, but that light was emitted from every part of the Moon's sunlit surface in all directions.[^cite_note-315] [Shen Kuo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shen_Kuo) (1031–1095) of the [Song dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_dynasty) created an allegory equating the [waxing and waning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waxing_and_waning) of the Moon to a round ball of reflective silver that, when doused with white powder and viewed from the side, would appear to be a crescent.[^cite_note-Needham1986-305]<sup>: 415–416 </sup> During the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), before the invention of the telescope, the Moon was increasingly recognized as a sphere, though many believed that it was "perfectly smooth".[^cite_note-316]

### Telescopic exploration (1609–1959)



The first published sketches of one of the first telescopic observations of the Moon, from the paper *[Sidereus Nuncius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidereus_Nuncius)* (1610) by [Galileo Galilei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galileo_Galilei), providing among other findings the first descriptions of the Moon's topography

The [telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telescope) was developed and reported on in 1608.[^cite_note-317] The first record of telescopic astronomy and rough mapping of the Moon's features is from early summer 1609 by [Thomas Harriot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Harriot), but which he did not publish.[^cite_note-318] At the same time [Galileo Galilei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galileo_Galilei) too started to use telescopes to observe the sky and the Moon, recording later that year more detailed observations and crucial conclusions, such as that the Moon was not smooth, featuring mountains and craters, which he published in 1610 in his ground-breaking and soon widely discussed book *[Sidereus Nuncius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidereus_Nuncius)*.[^cite_note-319]

Later in the 17th century, the efforts of [Giovanni Battista Riccioli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giovanni_Battista_Riccioli) and [Francesco Maria Grimaldi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francesco_Maria_Grimaldi) led to the system of naming of lunar features in use today.[^cite_note-320] The more exact 1834–1836 *[Mappa Selenographica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mappa_Selenographica)* of [Wilhelm Beer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_Beer) and [Johann Heinrich von Mädler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Heinrich_von_M%C3%A4dler), and their associated 1837 book *Der Mond*, the first [trigonometrically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trigonometry) accurate study of lunar features, included the heights of more than a thousand mountains, and introduced the study of the Moon at accuracies possible in earthly geography.[^cite_note-321] Lunar craters, first noted by Galileo, were thought to be [volcanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanic) until the 1870s proposal of [Richard Proctor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Proctor) that they were formed by collisions.[^cite_note-worldbook-90] This view gained support in 1892 from the experimentation of geologist [Grove Karl Gilbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grove_Karl_Gilbert), and from comparative studies from 1920 to the 1940s,[^cite_note-Hall1977-322] leading to the development of [lunar stratigraphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_geologic_timescale), which by the 1950s was becoming a new and growing branch of [astrogeology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrogeology).[^cite_note-worldbook-90]

### First missions to the Moon (1959–1976)

See also: [Space Race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Race) and [Moon landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_landing)

After [World War II](32927.md) the first [launch systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Launch_system) were developed and by the end of the 1950s they reached capabilities that allowed the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) and the [United States](3434750.md) to launch [spacecraft](37910.md) into space.[^cite_note-323] The [Cold War](325329.md) fueled a closely followed development of launch systems by the two states, resulting in the so-called [Space Race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Race) and its later phase the Moon Race, accelerating efforts and interest in [exploration of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exploration_of_the_Moon).[^cite_note-rmg-324]



First view of the [far side of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon), taken by [Luna 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_3), October 7, 1959. Clearly visible is [Mare Moscoviense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Moscoviense) (top right) and a mare triplet of [Mare Crisium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Crisium), [Mare Marginis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Marginis) and [Mare Smythii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mare_Smythii) (left center).

After the first spaceflight of [Sputnik 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputnik_1) in 1957 during [International Geophysical Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Geophysical_Year) the spacecraft of the Soviet Union's [*Luna* program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_programme) were the first to accomplish a number of goals. Following three unnamed failed missions in 1958,[^cite_note-325] the first human-made object *[Luna 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_1)* escaped Earth's gravity and passed the Moon on 4 January 1959.[^cite_note-NSSDCTrajectory-326] Later that year the first human-made object *[Luna 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_2)* reached the Moon's surface by [intentionally impacting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lander_\(spacecraft\)#Impactors).[^cite_note-327] By the end of the year *[Luna 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_3)* reached as the first human-made object the normally occluded [far side of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon), taking the first photographs of it.[^cite_note-328] The first spacecraft to perform a successful lunar [soft landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lander_\(spacecraft\)) was *[Luna 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_9)*[^cite_note-329] and the first vehicle to orbit the Moon was *[Luna 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_10)*, both in 1966.[^cite_note-worldbook-90]

The small blue-white semicircle of Earth, almost glowing with color in the blackness of space, rising over the limb of the desolate, cratered surface of the Moon.

*[Earthrise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthrise)*, by NASA astronaut [William Anders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Anders), the first color [image of Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_first_images_of_Earth_from_space) taken by a human from the Moon, during [Apollo 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_8) (1968), the first time a crewed spacecraft left Earth orbit and reached another [astronomical body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomical_body)

Following President [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy)'s 1961 commitment to a crewed Moon landing before the end of the decade, the United States, under NASA leadership, launched a series of uncrewed probes to develop an understanding of the lunar surface in preparation for human missions: the [Jet Propulsion Laboratory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_Propulsion_Laboratory)'s [Ranger program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranger_program), the [Lunar Orbiter program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_Orbiter_program) and the [Surveyor program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surveyor_program).[^cite_note-330] The crewed [Apollo program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_program) was developed in parallel; after a series of uncrewed and crewed tests of the Apollo spacecraft in Earth orbit, and spurred on by a potential [Soviet lunar human landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_human_lunar_programs), in 1968 [Apollo 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_8) made the first human mission to lunar orbit.[^cite_note-331]The first Earthlings, two tortoises, had circled the Moon three months earlier on the Soviet Union's [Zond 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zond_5), followed by turtles on [Zond 6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zond_6).[^cite_note-332]

The first time a person landed on the Moon and any extraterrestrial body was when [Neil Armstrong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Armstrong), the commander of the American mission [Apollo 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_11), set foot on the Moon at 02\:56 UTC on July 21, 1969.[^cite_note-333] Considered the culmination of the [Space Race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Race),[^cite_note-CNN-334] an estimated 500 million people worldwide watched the transmission by the [Apollo TV camera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_TV_camera), the largest television audience for a live broadcast at that time.[^cite_note-335][^cite_note-336] While at the same time another mission, the robotic sample return mission [Luna 15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_15) by the Soviet Union had been in orbit around the Moon, becoming together with Apollo 11 the first ever case of two extraterrestrial missions being conducted at the same time.[^cite_note-337]

The Apollo missions 11 to 17 (except [Apollo 13](1770.md), which aborted its planned lunar landing) removed 380.05 kilograms (837.87 lb) of lunar rock and soil in 2,196 [separate samples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_rock).[^cite_note-338] Scientific instrument packages were installed on the lunar surface during all the Apollo landings. Long-lived [instrument stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_Lunar_Surface_Experiments_Package), including heat flow probes, [seismometers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seismometer), and [magnetometers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetometer), were installed at the [Apollo 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_12), [14](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_14), [15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_15), [16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_16), and [17](1971.md) landing sites. Direct transmission of data to Earth concluded in late 1977 because of budgetary considerations,[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340] but as the stations' [lunar laser ranging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_laser_ranging) corner-cube retroreflector arrays are passive instruments, they are still being used.[^cite_note-341] Apollo 17 in 1972 was the last crewed mission to the Moon's orbit or surface, and the last to the Moon's vicinity until the [Artemis II](41391511.md) lunar flyby in 2026.[^cite_note-342] [Explorer 49](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Explorer_49) in 1973 was the last dedicated U.S. lunar probe until 1994.[^cite_note-343]



An example of the robotic and human exploration of the Moon in the first [visit of a separate mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_rendezvous) beyond [Low Earth Orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_Earth_Orbit) (1969),

[^cite_note-g936-344]

&#x20;[Apollo 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_12) astronaut [Pete Conrad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pete_Conrad) checking on [Surveyor 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surveyor_3) at their shared [lunar base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_base), with the Apollo 12 *Intrepid* lander and S-band antenna in the distant background

The Soviet Union continued sending robotic missions to the Moon until 1976, deploying in 1970 with [Luna 17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_17) the first remote controlled [rover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rover_\(space_exploration\)) [Lunokhod 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunokhod_1) on an extraterrestrial surface, and collecting and returning 0.3 kg of rock and soil samples with three *Luna* [sample return missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sample_return_mission) (*[Luna 16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_16)* in 1970, *[Luna 20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_20)* in 1972, and *[Luna 24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_24)* in 1976).[^cite_note-345]

### Moon Treaty and explorational absence (1976–1990)

Main article: [Moon Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Treaty)

Following the [last Soviet mission to the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_24) of 1976, there was little further lunar exploration for fourteen years.[^cite_note-timeline-346] Astronautics had shifted its focus towards the exploration of the [inner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_Solar_System) (e.g. [Venera program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venera_program)) and [outer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_Solar_System) (e.g. [Pioneer 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pioneer_10), 1972) [Solar System](26903.md) [planets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planet), but also towards [Earth orbit](568972.md), developing and continuously operating, beside [communication satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communication_satellite), [Earth observation satellites](46597.md) (e.g. [Landsat program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landsat_program), 1972), [space telescopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_telescope) and particularly [space stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_station) (e.g. [Salyut program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salyut_program), 1971).[^cite_note-347]

Negotiation in 1979 of [Moon treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_treaty), and its subsequent ratification in 1984 was the only major activity regarding the Moon until 1990.[^cite_note-348][^cite_note-timeline-346]

### Renewed exploration (1990–present)

In 1990 *[Hiten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiten_\(spacecraft\))* – *Hagoromo*,[^cite_note-349] the first dedicated lunar mission since 1976, reached the Moon's orbit. Sent by Japan, it became the first lunar mission that was not a Soviet Union or U.S. mission.[^cite_note-timeline-346]

In 1994, the U.S. dedicated a mission to fly a spacecraft (*[Clementine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clementine_\(spacecraft\))*) to the Moon again for the first time since 1973. This mission obtained the first near-global topographic map of the Moon, and the first global [multispectral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multispectral) images of the lunar surface.[^cite_note-350] In 1998, this was followed by the *[Lunar Prospector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_Prospector)* mission, whose instruments indicated the presence of excess hydrogen at the lunar poles, which is likely to have been caused by the presence of water ice in the upper few meters of the regolith within permanently shadowed craters.[^cite_note-351]

The next years saw a row of first missions to the Moon by a new group of states actively exploring the Moon. Between 2004 and 2006 the first spacecraft by the [European Space Agency](10363.md) (ESA) ([SMART-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SMART-1)) orbited the Moon, recording the first detailed survey of chemical elements on the lunar surface.[^cite_note-352] The [Chinese Lunar Exploration Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Lunar_Exploration_Program) reached the Moon for the first time with the orbiter *[Chang'e 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_1)* (2007–2009),[^cite_note-353] obtaining a full image map of the Moon. [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Space_Research_Organisation) reached, orbited, and impacted the Moon in 2008 for the first time with its [Chandrayaan-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandrayaan-1) and [Moon Impact Probe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Impact_Probe), becoming the fifth and sixth state to do so, creating a high-resolution chemical, mineralogical and photo-geological map of the lunar surface, and confirming the presence of [water molecules in lunar soil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_water).[^cite_note-354]

The U.S. launched the [Lunar Reconnaissance Orbiter (LRO)](15970718.md) and the [LCROSS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LCROSS) impactor on June 18, 2009. LCROSS completed its mission by making a planned and widely observed impact in the crater [Cabeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabeus) on October 9, 2009,[^cite_note-355] whereas *LRO* is currently in operation, obtaining precise lunar [altimetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altimetry) and high-resolution imagery.[^cite_note-356]

China continued its lunar program in 2010 with [Chang'e 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_2), mapping the surface at a higher resolution over an eight-month period, and in 2013 with [Chang'e 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_3), a lunar [lander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lander_\(spacecraft\)) along with a [lunar rover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_rover) named *[Yutu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yutu_\(rover\))* ([Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language): 玉兔; lit. 'Jade Rabbit'). This was the first lunar rover mission since *[Lunokhod 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunokhod_2)* in 1973 and the first lunar [soft landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_landing) since *[Luna 24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_24)* in 1976, making China the third country to achieve this.[^cite_note-357]

In 2014 the first privately funded probe, the [Manfred Memorial Moon Mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manfred_Memorial_Moon_Mission), flew by the Moon. Another Chinese rover mission, [Chang'e 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_4), achieved the first landing on [the Moon's far side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon) in early 2019.[^cite_note-358] Also in 2019, India successfully sent its second probe, [Chandrayaan-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandrayaan-2), to the Moon. In 2020, China carried out its first robotic [sample return mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sample_return_mission) ([Chang'e 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_5)), bringing back 1,731 grams of lunar material to Earth.[^cite_note-359]

The U.S. [developed plans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vision_for_Space_Exploration) for returning astronauts to the Moon beginning in 2004,[^cite_note-360] and with the signing of the U.S.-led [Artemis Accords](63968963.md) in 2020, the [Artemis program](60758751.md) aims to return humans to the Moon in the 2020s.[^cite_note-361] The Accords have been joined by a growing number of countries. The introduction of the Artemis Accords has fueled a renewed discussion about the international framework and cooperation of lunar activity, building on the [Moon Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Treaty) and the ESA-led [Moon Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Village) concept.[^cite_note-The_Space_Review_2020-362][^cite_note-Australian_Institute_of_International_Affairs_2021-363][^cite_note-The_Space_Treaty_Institute_–_Dedicated_to_Peace_and_Sustainability_in_Outer_Space._Our_Mission-364]

In 2022, South Korea launched [Danuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danuri) successfully, its first lunar probe, from the US..mw-parser-output
