(prefers-color-scheme\:dark){html.skin-theme-clientpref-os .mw-parser-output .cs1-maint{color:#18911f}}["Danuri, South Korea's first Moon mission"](https://www.planetary.org/space-missions/kplo). The Planetary Society. Retrieved September 21, 2026. In 2023 and 2024, India and Japan became the fourth and fifth country to [soft land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_landing) a spacecraft on the Moon, following the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) and United States in the 1960s, and China in 2013.[^cite_note-365] Notably, Japan's spacecraft, the [Smart Lander for Investigating Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smart_Lander_for_Investigating_Moon), survived three lunar nights.[^cite_note-366] In February 2024, the [IM-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IM-1) lander became the first commercially built lander to land on the Moon.[^cite_note-367]

China launched [Chang'e 6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_6) in May 2024, which conducted the first sample return from the [far side of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_side_of_the_Moon).[^cite_note-AJ_FI-20230425-368] It also carried a Chinese rover to conduct [infrared spectroscopy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absorption_spectroscopy) of lunar surface.[^cite_note-369] [Pakistan](23235.md) sent a lunar orbiter called [ICUBE-Q](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICUBE-Q) along with Chang'e 6.[^cite_note-370] [Nova-C 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IM-2), [iSpace Lander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ispace_\(Japanese_company\)#Hakuto-R_program) and [Blue Ghost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Ghost) were all launched to the Moon in 2024.[^cite_note-371]



Photograph of the Moon, along with Earth during *[Earthset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthset)* and the capsule *[ Integrity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orion_\(spacecraft\))*, from [cislunar space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cislunar_space) during the crewed [Artemis II](41391511.md) [lunar flyby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_flyby) in 2026, the first crewed circumlunar flight since 1972

NASA's [Artemis II](41391511.md) crewed lunar flyby mission launched in April 2026. It was the first crewed mission around the Moon since the Apollo era. [Christina Koch](39700916.md) became the first woman, [Victor Glover](39700888.md) the first person of color, Canadian [Jeremy Hansen](22811438.md) the first non-American,[^cite_note-372] and commander [Reid Wiseman](23437180.md) the oldest person, to leave [low Earth orbit](47568.md) and to fly around the Moon.[^cite_note-373]

### Future

See also: [List of proposed missions to the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_proposed_missions_to_the_Moon)

Both the U.S. and Chinese lunar programs have the goal to establish a [lunar base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_base) in the 2030s with their international partners, being the first crewed lunar landings since [Apollo 17](1971.md) in 1972.[^cite_note-Since_Apollo_17-374]

Following the [Artemis III](50493439.md) test flight in [low Earth orbit](47568.md) scheduled for 2027, NASA plans to return humans to the lunar surface in 2028, with two [Artemis IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_IV) astronauts landing on the lunar south pole.[^cite_note-Since_Apollo_17-374] While the Apollo missions were explorational in nature, the Artemis program plans to establish a more permanent presence. To this end, the U.S. is partnering with industry for lunar communications, payload delivery, and [in situ resource utilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_situ_resource_utilization) through programs such as NASA's [Commercial Lunar Payload Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_Lunar_Payload_Services), and [DARPA lunar programs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DARPA_lunar_programs).[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377]

China is continuing its ambitious [Chang'e program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_program), having announced joint missions with Russia's struggling [Luna-Glob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna-Glob) program.[^cite_note-TASS_2019_x202-378][^cite_note-379] Two lunar survey missions are planned, [Chang'e 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_7) in 2026 and [Chang'e 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_8) in 2028, before a crewed landing in 2029 or 2030.[^cite_note-380][^cite_note-381]

## Human interaction and presence

See also: [Human presence in space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_presence_in_space)



Map of all the sites of [soft landings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_landing) on the Moon (2024)

Human presence on the Moon began with [remains](537458.md) of impactors, landings and [lunar orbiters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_extraterrestrial_orbiters#Moon). Orbiters like the 2009-launched [Lunar Reconnaissance Orbiter](15970718.md) continue to surveil the Moon for future missions, while landers such as the 2013-launched [Chang'e 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'e_3) with its Lunar Ultraviolet Telescope are still operational as of 2020.[^cite_note-382] Five retroreflectors have been installed on the Moon since the 1970s and since used for accurate measurements of the physical [librations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libration) through [laser ranging to the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_Laser_Ranging_experiment).[^cite_note-UniverseToday-383]

### Human impact

See also: [Space debris](266344.md), [Space sustainability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_sustainability), [List of artificial objects on the Moon](537458.md), [Space art § Art in space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_art#Art_in_space), [Moonbase](8420417.md), [Lunar resources § Mining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_resources#Mining), [Tourism on the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_on_the_Moon), and [Space archaeology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_archaeology)



Artifacts of human activity, [Apollo 17's Lunar Surface Experiments Package](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_Lunar_Surface_Experiments_Package)

[^cite_note-Garber_2012-384]

While the Moon has the lowest [planetary protection target-categorization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_protection#Target_categories), its degradation as a pristine body and scientific place has been discussed.[^cite_note-Vidaurri_2019-385] If there is [astronomy performed from the Moon](#Astronomy_from_the_Moon), it will need to be free from any physical and [radio pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_pollution). While the Moon has no significant atmosphere, traffic and impacts on the Moon causes clouds of dust that can spread far and possibly contaminate the original state of the Moon and its special scientific content.[^cite_note-David_2020-386] Scholar [Alice Gorman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alice_Gorman) asserts that, although the Moon is inhospitable, describing the Moon as dead negates its dynamics, which requires sustainable human activity to treat the Moon's ecology as a co-participant.[^cite_note-Gorman_2022-387]

The so-called "[Tardigrade affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tardigrades_on_the_Moon)" of the 2019 crashed *[Beresheet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beresheet)* lander and its carrying of [tardigrades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tardigrade) has been discussed as an example for lacking measures and lacking international regulation for [planetary protection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_protection).[^cite_note-Alvarez_2020_p.-388]

[Space debris](266344.md) beyond Earth around the Moon has been considered as a future challenge with increasing numbers of missions to the Moon, particularly as a danger for such missions.[^cite_note-Carter_2022-389][^cite_note-Nast_2013-390] As such lunar waste management has been raised as an issue which future lunar missions, particularly on the surface, need to tackle.[^cite_note-Pino_Salmeri_Hugo_Hume_p.-391][^cite_note-NASA/ADS-392]

Human remains have been transported to the Moon, including by private companies such as [Celestis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celestis) and [Elysium Space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elysium_Space). Because the Moon has been sacred or significant to many cultures, the practice of [space burials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_burial) have attracted criticism from [indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples) leaders. For example, then–[Navajo Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navajo_Nation) president [Albert Hale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Hale) criticized NASA for sending the [cremated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cremated) ashes of scientist [Eugene Shoemaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugene_Shoemaker) to the Moon in 1998.[^cite_note-Magazine_Sullivan_2024_a329-393][^cite_note-web.archive.org_2011_c901-394]

Beside the remains of human activity on the Moon, there have been some intended permanent installations like the *[Moon Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Museum)* art piece, [Apollo 11 goodwill messages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_11_goodwill_messages), six [lunar plaques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_plaque), the *[Fallen Astronaut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fallen_Astronaut)* memorial, and other artifacts.[^cite_note-Garber_2012-384]

### Astronomy from the Moon

Further information: [Extraterrestrial sky § The Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extraterrestrial_sky#The_Moon)



Earth's [exosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exosphere) illuminated creating its [geocorona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geocorona), visible in ultraviolet and viewed by the [Far Ultraviolet Camera/Spectrograph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_Ultraviolet_Camera/Spectrograph) of [Apollo 16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_16) in 1972 from the Moon's surface

The Moon has been used as a site for astronomical and [Earth observations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_observation). The Earth appears in the [Moon's sky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extraterrestrial_sky#The_Moon) with an [apparent size](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apparent_size) of 1° 48′ to 2°,[^cite_note-Gorkavyi_Krotkov_Marshak_2023_pp._1527–1537-395] three to four times the size of the Moon or Sun in Earth's sky, or about the apparent width of two little fingers at an arm's length away. Observations from the Moon started as early as 1966 with the [first images of Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_first_images_of_Earth_from_space) from the Moon, taken by [Lunar Orbiter 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_Orbiter_1). Of particular cultural significance is the 1968 photograph called *[Earthrise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthrise)*, taken by [Bill Anders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Anders) of [Apollo 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_8) in 1968. In April 1972 the [Apollo 16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_16) mission set up the first dedicated telescope,[^cite_note-w422-396][^cite_note-x909-397] the [Far Ultraviolet Camera/Spectrograph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_Ultraviolet_Camera/Spectrograph), recording various astronomical photos and spectra.[^cite_note-398]

The Moon is recognized as an excellent site for telescopes.[^cite_note-399] It is relatively nearby; certain craters near the poles are permanently dark and cold and especially useful for [infrared telescopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrared_telescope); and [radio telescopes](46656.md) on the far side would be shielded from the radio chatter of Earth.[^cite_note-400] The [lunar soil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_soil), although it poses a problem for any moving parts of [telescopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telescope), can be mixed with [carbon nanotubes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_nanotube) and [epoxies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epoxy) and employed in the construction of mirrors up to 50 meters in diameter.[^cite_note-401] A lunar [zenith telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zenith_telescope) can be made cheaply with an [ionic liquid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionic_liquid).[^cite_note-402]

With increased human activity on the surface of the Moon the atmospheric circulation of lunar dust will increase, reducing the favorable conditions for astronomy from the surface depending on measures to mitigate the spread of lunar dust.[^cite_note-y615-403]

### Living on the Moon

Main article: [Lunar habitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_habitation)



Astronaut [Buzz Aldrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buzz_Aldrin) in [life-supporting suit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo/Skylab_spacesuit) looking back at the first [lunar habitat and base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_base), the [Lunar Module *Eagle*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_Module_Eagle) of [Tranquility Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tranquility_Base), during [Apollo 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_11) (1969), the first crewed [Moon landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_landing)

Humans have lived on the Moon in groups of two and for up to three days.[^cite_note-i632-404] Altogether twelve people have stayed on the Moon over the course of six visits.[^cite_note-n138-405] They all lived in one type of [surface habitat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_habitat_\(facility\)#Extraterrestrial_surface_habitat), the [Apollo Lunar Module](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_Lunar_Module).[^cite_note-406] In the course of these stays some people have spent in total up to one day roaming the surface.[^cite_note-i632-404]

Challenges when roaming the surface arise from [lunar dust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_dust) sticking to suits and tools, and has been carried inside the habitats. Astronauts could taste and smell the dust, which smells like gunpowder and was called the "Apollo aroma".[^cite_note-Aroma-407] This fine lunar dust can [cause health issues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_dust#Harmful_effects_of_lunar_dust).[^cite_note-Aroma-407]

In 2019, at least one plant seed sprouted in an experiment on the [Chang'e 4 lander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Change_4#Lunar_lander). It was carried from Earth along with other small life in its *Lunar Micro Ecosystem*.[^cite_note-Seeds-408]

Because the Moon is tidally locked, and only shows one face to Earth, a permanent lunar presence will require a constellation of satellites to relay signals from the Moon's far side, as well as over its topographically inhibitive south pole. The Chinese [Queqiao-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queqiao-1) [and -2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queqiao-2) satellites are already in place, and NASA plans the [Lunar Communications Relay and Navigation Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LunaNet) (LCRNS) for November 2026.[^cite_note-409][^cite_note-410][^cite_note-411][^cite_note-412][^cite_note-413][^cite_note-414]

### Legal status

See also: [Space law](743972.md), [Politics of outer space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_outer_space), [Space advocacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_advocacy), [Colonization of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonization_of_the_Moon), [Outer Space Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_Space_Treaty), and [Moon Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Treaty)

Although *[Luna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_program)* landers scattered pennants of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) on the Moon, and [U.S. flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_Flag_Assembly) were symbolically planted at their landing sites by the [Apollo astronauts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_astronauts), no nation claims ownership of any part of the Moon's surface.[^cite_note-unoosa_q6-415] Likewise no [private ownership of parts of the Moon, or as a whole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extraterrestrial_real_estate), is considered credible.[^cite_note-unoosa_q7-416][^cite_note-iisl_2004-417][^cite_note-iisl_2009-418]

The 1967 [Outer Space Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_Space_Treaty) defines the Moon and all outer space as the "[province of all mankind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_heritage_of_mankind)".[^cite_note-unoosa_q6-415] It restricts the use of the Moon to peaceful purposes, explicitly banning military installations and [weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapons_of_mass_destruction).[^cite_note-unoosa_q5-419] A majority of countries are parties of this treaty.[^cite_note-unoosa_q4-420] The 1979 [Moon Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Agreement) was created to elaborate, and restrict the exploitation of the [Moon's resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_resources) by any single nation, leaving it to a yet unspecified international regulatory regime.[^cite_note-The_Space_Review_2021-421] As of January 2020, it has been signed and ratified by 18 nations,[^cite_note-unoosa_moon-422] none of which have [human spaceflight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_spaceflight) capabilities.

Since 2020, countries have joined the U.S. in their [Artemis Accords](63968963.md), which are challenging the treaty. The U.S. has furthermore emphasized in a presidential [executive order](210541.md) ("Encouraging International Support for the Recovery and Use of Space Resources.") that "the United States does not view outer space as a 'global commons'" and calls the Moon Agreement "a failed attempt at constraining free enterprise".[^cite_note-423][^cite_note-424]

With Australia signing and ratifying both the Moon Treaty in 1986 as well as the Artemis Accords in 2020, there has been a discussion if they can be harmonized.[^cite_note-Australian_Institute_of_International_Affairs_2021-363] In this light an *Implementation Agreement* for the Moon Treaty has been advocated for, as a way to compensate for the shortcomings of the Moon Treaty and to harmonize it with other laws and agreements such as the Artemis Accords, allowing it to be more widely accepted.[^cite_note-The_Space_Review_2020-362][^cite_note-The_Space_Treaty_Institute_–_Dedicated_to_Peace_and_Sustainability_in_Outer_Space._Our_Mission-364]

In the face of such increasing commercial and national interest, particularly prospecting territories, U.S. lawmakers have introduced in late 2020 specific regulation for the conservation of historic landing sites[^cite_note-HowStuffWorks_2021-425] and interest groups have argued for making such sites [World Heritage Sites](44940.md)[^cite_note-For_All_Moonkind-426] and zones of scientific value protected zones, all of which add to the legal availability and territorialization of the Moon.[^cite_note-Alvarez_2020_p.-388]

In 2021, the *Declaration of the Rights of the Moon*[^cite_note-Declaration-427] was created by a group of "lawyers, space archaeologists and concerned citizens", drawing on precedents in the [Rights of Nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rights_of_Nature) movement and the concept of legal personality for non-human entities in space.[^cite_note-428][^cite_note-Evans_2021-429]

#### Coordination and regulation

Increasing human activity at the Moon has raised the need for coordination to safeguard international and commercial lunar activity. Issues from cooperation to mere coordination, through for example the development of a shared [Lunar time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_time), have been raised.

In particular the establishment of an [international](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internationalism_\(politics\)) or [United Nations](31769.md) regulatory regime for lunar human activity has been called for by the [Moon Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Treaty) and suggested through an *Implementation Agreement*,[^cite_note-The_Space_Review_2020-362][^cite_note-The_Space_Treaty_Institute_–_Dedicated_to_Peace_and_Sustainability_in_Outer_Space._Our_Mission-364] but remains contentious. Current lunar programs are [multilateral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multilateralism), with the US-led [Artemis program](60758751.md) and the China-led [International Lunar Research Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Lunar_Research_Station). For broader international cooperation and coordination, the [International Lunar Exploration Working Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Lunar_Exploration_Working_Group) (ILEWG), the [Moon Village Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Village) (MVA) and more generally the International Space Exploration Coordination Group (ISECG) has been established.

## In culture and life

### Timekeeping

Further information: [Lunar calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_calendar), [Lunisolar calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunisolar_calendar), and [Metonic cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metonic_cycle)



The [Venus of Laussel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venus_of_Laussel) (c. 25,000 [BP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Before_present)) holding a crescent shaped horn. The 13 notches on the horn may symbolize the average number of days from menstruation to an [ovulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ovulation), or the approximate number of full [menstrual cycles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menstrual_cycle) and [lunar cycles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_cycle) per year (although these two phenomena are unrelated).

[^cite_note-\:0b-430]

[^cite_note-e093-293]

Since pre-historic times people have taken note of [the Moon's phases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_phases) and its [waxing and waning cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_cycle) and used it to keep record of time. [Tally sticks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tally_stick), notched bones dating as far back as 20–30,000 years ago, are believed by some to mark the phases of the Moon.[^cite_note-Burton2011-294][^cite_note-431][^cite_note-432] The counting of the days between the Moon's phases eventually gave rise to generalized [time periods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unit_of_time) of lunar cycles as [months](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Month), and possibly of its phases as [weeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Week).[^cite_note-Zerubavel_1989_p._9-433]

The words for the month in a range of different languages carry this relation between the period of the month and the Moon etymologically. The English *month* as well as *moon*, and its cognates in other Indo-European languages (e.g. the [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) *mensis* and [Ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek) μείς (*meis*) or μήν (mēn), meaning "month")[^cite_note-434][^cite_note-435][^cite_note-436][^cite_note-437] stem from the [Proto-Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Indo-European) (PIE) root of *moon*, \**méh<sub>1</sub>nōt*, derived from the PIE verbal root \**meh<sub>1</sub>*-, "to measure", "indicat\[ing] a functional conception of the Moon, i.e. marker of the month" ([cf.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cf.) the English words *measure* and *menstrual*).[^cite_note-438][^cite_note-439][^cite_note-440] To give another example from a different [language family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_family), the [Chinese language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language) uses the same word ([月](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E6%9C%88)) for *moon* as for *month*, which furthermore can be found in the symbols for the word *week* ([星期](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E6%98%9F%E6%9C%9F)).[^cite_note-441]

This lunar timekeeping gave rise to the historically dominant, but varied, [lunisolar calendars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunisolar_calendar). The 7th-century [Islamic calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_calendar) is an example of a purely [lunar calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_calendar), where months are traditionally determined by the visual sighting of the hilal, or earliest crescent moon, over the horizon.[^cite_note-442]

Of particular significance has been the occasion of [full moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_moon), highlighted and celebrated in a range of calendars and cultures, an example being the Buddhist [Vesak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vesak). The full moon around the [southern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_equinox) or [northern autumnal equinox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_equinox) is often called the [harvest moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvest_moon) and is celebrated with festivities such as the [Harvest Moon Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvest_Moon_Festival) of the [Chinese lunar calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_lunar_calendar), its second most important celebration after [the Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_New_Year) lunisolar [Lunar New Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_New_Year).[^cite_note-Confucius_Institute_for_Scotland_2022-443]

In [ancient Egyptian religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egyptian_religion), [Khonsu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khonsu) was the god both of the Moon and the passage of time, and his worship was heavily tied to the Egyptian lunar calendar.[^cite_note-444]

### Cultural representation

Further information: [Cultural astronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_astronomy), [Archaeoastronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeoastronomy), [Lunar deity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_deity), [Selene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selene), [Luna (goddess)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_\(goddess\)), [Crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crescent), and [Man in the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man_in_the_Moon)

See also: [Nocturne (painting)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nocturne_\(painting\)) and [Moon magic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_magic)

Recurring lunar [aspects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aspect_\(religion\)) of [lunar deities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_deities)

Sumerian cylinder seal and impression, dated c. 2100 BC, of Ḫašḫamer, ensi (governor) of Iškun-Sin c. 2100 BC. The seated figure is probably king Ur-Nammu, bestowing the governorship on Ḫašḫamer, who is led before him by Lamma (protective goddess).

The [crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crescent) of [Nanna/Sîn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sin_\(mythology\)), c. 2100 BC

Luna on the Parabiago plate (2nd–5th century), featuring the crescent crown, chariot and velificatio as lunar aspect found in different cultures.

Crescent headgear, [chariot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_\(goddess\)#Chariot_of_the_Moon) and [velificatio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Velificatio) of [Luna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_\(goddess\)), 2nd–5th century

Rabbits are in a range of cultures identified with the Moon, from China to the Indigenous peoples of the Americas, as with the rabbit (on the left) of the Maya moon goddess (6th–9th century).

A [Moon rabbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_rabbit) of the [Maya moon goddess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_moon_goddess), 6th–9th century

Humans have not only observed the Moon since [prehistoric times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_times), but have also developed intricate perceptions of the Moon. Over time the Moon has been characterized and associated in many different ways, from having a [spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spirit_\(vital_essence\)) or being [a deity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_deity), and an [aspect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aspect_\(religion\)) thereof or an aspect [in astrology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_\(astrology\)), being made an important part of many [cosmologies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmology).

This rich history of humans viewing the Moon has been evidenced starting with depictions from 40,000 [BP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Before_present) and in written form from the [4th millennium BCE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/4th_millennium_BCE) in the earliest cases of [writing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writing). The oldest named astronomer and poet [Enheduanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enheduanna), [Akkadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_Empire) high priestess to the lunar deity [Nanna/Sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sin_\(mythology\)) and daughter of [Sargon the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sargon_the_Great) (c. 2334 – c. 2279 BCE), tracked the Moon and wrote poems about her.[^cite_note-c099-298]

#### Crescent

For the representation of the Moon, especially its [lunar phases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_phase), the [crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crescent) (🌙) has been a recurring symbol in a range of cultures since at least 3,000 BCE or possibly earlier with bull horns dating to the earliest [cave paintings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cave_painting) at 40,000 [BP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Before_present).[^cite_note-e093-293][^cite_note-k874-300] In [writing systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writing_system) such as Chinese the crescent has developed into the symbol [月](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E6%9C%88), the word for Moon, and in ancient Egyptian it was the symbol [𓇹](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%F0%93%87%B9), meaning Moon and spelled like the ancient Egyptian lunar deity *[Iah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iah)*,[^cite_note-Hart_2005_p._77-446] which the other ancient Egyptian lunar deities [Khonsu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khonsu) and [Thoth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thoth) were associated with.

Iconographically the crescent was used in [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia) as the primary symbol of [Nanna/Sîn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sin_\(mythology\)),[^cite_note-BlackGreen1992-297] the ancient [Sumerian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumeria) lunar deity,[^cite_note-Nemet1998-447][^cite_note-BlackGreen1992-297] who was the father of [Inanna/Ishtar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inanna), the goddess of the planet [Venus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venus) (symbolized as the [eight pointed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octagram) [Star of Ishtar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_of_Ishtar)),[^cite_note-Nemet1998-447][^cite_note-BlackGreen1992-297] and [Utu/Shamash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utu), the god of the Sun ([symbolized as a disc, optionally with eight rays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_symbol)),[^cite_note-Nemet1998-447][^cite_note-BlackGreen1992-297] all three often depicted next to each other. Nanna/Sîn is, like some other lunar deities, for example Iah and Khonsu of ancient Egypt, [Mene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mene_\(goddess\))/[Selene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selene) of ancient Greece and [Luna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_\(mythology\)) of ancient Rome, depicted as a [horned deity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horned_deity), featuring crescent shaped headgears or crowns.[^cite_note-448][^cite_note-449]

The particular arrangement of the crescent with a star known as the [star and crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_and_crescent) (☪️) goes back to the Bronze Age, representing either the Sun and Moon, or the Moon and the planet Venus, in combination. It came to represent the selene goddess [Artemis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis), and via the patronage of [Hecate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hecate), which as [triple deity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_deity) under the [epithet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epithet) *trimorphos*/*trivia* included aspects of Artemis/[Diana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_\(mythology\)), came to be used as a [symbol of Byzantium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_flags_and_insignia), with [Virgin Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Mary) ([Queen of Heaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_of_Heaven)) later taking her place, becoming depicted in [Marian veneration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marian_veneration) on a crescent and adorned with stars. Since then the [heraldric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heraldry) use of the star and crescent proliferated, Byzantium's symbolism possibly influencing the development of the [Ottoman flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_flag), specifically the combination of the Turkish crescent with a star,[^cite_note-450] and becoming a popular [symbol for Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbols_of_Islam) (as the *[hilal](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/hilal#Noun)* of the [Islamic calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_calendar)) and [for a range of nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_and_crescent#Contemporary_use).[^cite_note-Kadoi_2014-451]

#### Other associations

The features of the Moon, the contrasting brighter highlands and darker maria, have been seen by different cultures [forming abstract shapes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_pareidolia). Such shapes are among others the [Man in the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man_in_the_Moon) (e.g. [Coyolxāuhqui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coyolx%C4%81uhqui)) or the [Moon Rabbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Rabbit) (e.g. the Chinese [Tu'er Ye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tu'er_Ye) or in [Indigenous American mythologies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mythologies_of_the_indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas) the aspect of the [Maya moon goddess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_moon_goddess), from which possibly [Awilix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Awilix) is derived, or of [Metztli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metztli)/[Tēcciztēcatl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C4%93ccizt%C4%93catl)).[^cite_note-Collections_Search_–_Museum_of_Fine_Arts,_Boston_1987-445]

Occasionally some lunar deities have been also depicted [driving a chariot across the sky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luna_\(goddess\)#Chariot_of_the_Moon), such as the Hindu [Chandra/Soma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandra), the Greek Artemis, which is associated with Selene, or Luna, Selene's ancient Roman equivalent. Throughout the global Middle Ages, the moon held an important cultural role in topics as wide-ranging as [love poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Love_poetry), [prophecy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophecy), medical thinking.[^cite_note-452]

Color and material wise the Moon has been associated in Western [alchemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alchemy) with [silver](27119.md), while gold is associated with the Sun.[^cite_note-Abbri_2019_pp._39–44-453]

&#x20;Through a miracle, the so-called [splitting of the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Splitting_of_the_Moon) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): انشقاق القمر) in [Islam](6037917.md), association with the Moon applies also to [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad).[^cite_note-454]

### Representation in modern culture

See also: [Moon in science fiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_in_science_fiction) and [List of appearances of the Moon in fiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_appearances_of_the_Moon_in_fiction)



The Moon is prominently featured in [Vincent van Gogh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vincent_van_Gogh)'s 1889 painting *[The Starry Night](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Starry_Night)*.

[^cite_note-The_New_York_Times_2019-455]



An iconic image of the [Man in the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man_in_the_Moon) from the first [science-fiction film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science-fiction_film) set in space, *[A Trip to the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Trip_to_the_Moon)* (1902, [Georges Méliès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_M%C3%A9li%C3%A8s)), inspired by a history of literature about going to the Moon.

The perception of the Moon in the [modern era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_era) has been informed by [telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telescope)-enabled [modern astronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_astronomy). These new insights inspired cultural references, connecting romantic reflections about the Moon[^cite_note-The_MIT_Press_Reader_2020-456] and speculative fiction such as science-fiction dealing with the Moon.[^cite_note-The_New_York_Times_2019-455][^cite_note-Nature_2019-457]

Later [spaceflight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spaceflight) ushered in the [space age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_age), which produced especially with the first [Moon landings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_landing) historic achievements and references for [global society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_society), represented through [images of Earth from a distance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_image_of_Earth) and summarized in an awe and awareness called the [overview effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overview_effect), amounting to [Moon joy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_joy) by reaching the Moon.

Video of the first step on the surface of the Moon, performed by [Neil Armstrong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Armstrong) and commented with "That's one small step for \[a] man, one giant leap for mankind.", which was broadcast live, internationally (1969, [Apollo 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_11)).



The much reproduced image of [Buzz Aldrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buzz_Aldrin) (Apollo 11), with Neil Armstrong and Earth reflected in his visor.

[^cite_note-Byrne._2019-458]

This has been accompanied by public and critical reflection on humanity's cultural and [legal relation to the celestial body](#Legal_status), particularly as an object of [imperialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperialism), as in the [space race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_race), and as an object of [colonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_colonization#Colonialism) through [lunar resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_resources) [commercialization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercialization_of_space).[^cite_note-Alvarez_2020_p.-388] The 1970 poem "[Whitey on the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitey_on_the_Moon)" has become famous for its expression of such critical reflections.[^cite_note-x071-459] Other reflections have invoked the Moon's nature,[^cite_note-Declaration-427] raising issues like lunar conservation and [sustainability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_sustainability),[^cite_note-Nast_2013-390] understanding the Moon as a [common](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commons).[^cite_note-Open_Lunar_Foundation_2022-460][^cite_note-The_Space_Review_2021-421][^cite_note-Evans_2021-429]

In 2021 the 20 July became the *International Moon Day*, the date of the [first crewed Moon landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_11#Landing).[^cite_note-Nations_1967_a849-461]

### Lunar effect

Main article: [Lunar effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_effect)

The lunar effect is a supposed correlation between specific stages of the roughly 29.5-day lunar cycle, and behavior and physiological changes in humans on Earth. Lunar cycles have significant impacts on human culture, but no solid evidence connects these cycles to human biology.[^cite_note-FosterRoenneberg2008-462]

The Moon has long been associated with insanity and irrationality; the words *lunacy* and *[lunatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunatic)* are derived from the Latin name for the Moon, *Luna*. Philosophers [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle) and [Pliny the Elder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pliny_the_Elder) argued that the full moon induced insanity in susceptible individuals, believing that the brain, which is mostly water, must be affected by the Moon and its power over the tides, but the Moon's gravity is too slight to affect any single person.[^cite_note-sciam-463] In modern society lunar effects have been claimed to be responsible for supposed increased admissions to psychiatric hospitals, traffic accidents, homicides or suicides during a full moon,[^cite_note-sciam-463] but these claims have been invalidated by at least 37 studies.[^cite_note-RottonKelly1985-464]

These are [quasi-satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quasi-satellite) – they are not moons as they do not orbit Earth. For more information, see [Other moons of Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Other_moons_of_Earth).

[^cite_note-maxval-17]: &#x20;The *maximum value* is given based on scaling of the brightness from the value of −12.74 given for an equator to Moon-center distance of 378 000 km in the NASA factsheet reference to the minimum Earth–Moon distance given there, after the latter is corrected for Earth's equatorial radius of 6 378 km, giving 350 600 km. The *minimum value* (for a distant [new moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_moon)) is based on a similar scaling using the maximum Earth–Moon distance of 407 000 km (given in the factsheet) and by calculating the brightness of the [earthshine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthshine) onto such a new moon. The brightness of the earthshine is \[ Earth [albedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albedo) × ([Earth radius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_radius) / Radius of [Moon's orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon's_orbit))<sup>2</sup> ] relative to the direct solar illumination that occurs for a full moon. (Earth albedo = 0.367; Earth radius = (polar radius × equatorial radius)<sup>½</sup> = 6 367 km.)

[^cite_note-angular_size-19]: &#x20;The range of angular size values given are based on simple scaling of the following values given in the fact sheet reference: at an Earth-equator to Moon-center distance of 378 000 km, the [angular size](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angular_size) is 1896 [arcseconds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arcsecond). The same fact sheet gives extreme Earth–Moon distances of 407 000 km and 357 000 km. For the maximum angular size, the minimum distance has to be corrected for Earth's equatorial radius of 6 378 km, giving 350 600 km.

[^cite_note-pressure_explanation-21]: &#x20;Lucey et al. (2006) give 10<sup>7</sup> particles cm<sup>−3</sup> by day and 10<sup>5</sup> particles cm<sup>−3</sup> by night. Along with equatorial surface temperatures of 390 [K](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelvin) by day and 100 K by night, the [ideal gas law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideal_gas_law) yields the pressures given in the infobox (rounded to the nearest [order of magnitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_magnitude)): 10<sup>−7</sup> [Pa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pascal_\(unit\)) by day and 10<sup>−10</sup> Pa by night.

[^cite_note-91]: &#x20;There is no strong correlation between the sizes of planets and the sizes of their satellites. Larger planets tend to have more satellites, both large and small, than smaller planets.

[^cite_note-Moon_vs._Charon-92]: &#x20;With 27% the diameter and 60% the density of Earth, the Moon has 1.23% of the mass of Earth. The moon [Charon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charon_\(moon\)) is larger relative to its primary [Pluto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluto), but Earth and the Moon are different since Pluto is considered a [dwarf planet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwarf_planet) and not a planet, unlike Earth.

[^cite_note-distance_ratio-220]: &#x20;The mean distance of the Moon from the Earth is     384401   {\displaystyle 384401}  {\displaystyle 384401} km. The equatorial radius of Earth is     6.378136 ⋅  10  6     {\displaystyle 6.378136\cdot 10^{6}}  {\displaystyle 6.378136\cdot 10^{6}}[meters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metre). Multiplying by     2 π   {\displaystyle 2\pi }  {\displaystyle 2\pi } to convert from equatorial radius to equatorial circumference results in     4.007501 ⋅  10  7     {\displaystyle 4.007501\cdot 10^{7}}  {\displaystyle 4.007501\cdot 10^{7}} [meters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metre), or     40075.01 k m   {\displaystyle 40075.01km}  {\displaystyle 40075.01km}. Dividing the two results in a ratio of     9.59   {\displaystyle 9.59}  {\displaystyle 9.59}.

[^cite_note-orbpd-224]: &#x20;More accurately, the Moon's mean sidereal period (fixed star to fixed star) is 27.321661 days (27 d 07 h 43 min 11.5 s), and its mean tropical orbital period (from equinox to equinox) is 27.321582 days (27 d 07 h 43 min 04.7 s) (*Explanatory Supplement to the Astronomical Ephemeris*, 1961, at p.107).

[^cite_note-synpd-226]: &#x20;More accurately, the Moon's mean synodic period (between mean solar conjunctions) is 29.530589 days (29 d 12 h 44 min 02.9 s) (*Explanatory Supplement to the Astronomical Ephemeris*, 1961, at p.107).

[^cite_note-size_changes-286]: &#x20;See graph in [Sun#Life phases](26751.md#Life_phases). At present, the diameter of the Sun is increasing at a rate of about five percent per billion years. This is very similar to the rate at which the apparent angular diameter of the Moon is decreasing as it recedes from Earth.

[^cite_note-area-290]: &#x20;On average, the Moon covers an area of 0.21078 square degrees on the night sky.

[^cite_note-:0-119]: &#x20;Tikoo, Sonia M.; Weiss, Benjamin P.; Shuster, David L.; Suavet, Clément; Wang, Huapei; Grove, Timothy L. (August 4, 2017). ["A two-billion-year history for the lunar dynamo"](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5550224). *Science Advances*. **3** (8) e1700207. [Bibcode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibcode_\(identifier\)):[2017SciA....3E0207T](https://ui.adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/2017SciA....3E0207T). [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1126/sciadv.1700207](https://doi.org/10.1126%2Fsciadv.1700207). [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\)) [2375-2548](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/2375-2548). [PMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMC_\(identifier\)) [5550224](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5550224). [PMID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMID_\(identifier\)) [28808679](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28808679). No single dynamo mechanism proposed thus far can explain the strong fields inferred for the period before 3.56 Ga while also allowing the dynamo to persist in such a weakened state beyond \~2.5 Ga. Therefore, our results suggest that the dynamo was powered by at least two distinct mechanisms operating during early and late lunar history.

It has now been established that a dynamo magnetic field likely existed on the Moon from at least 4.5 billion to 3.56 billion years ago, with an intensity similar to that at the surface of Earth today.

[^cite_note-:3-121]: &#x20;Mighani, Saied; Wang, Huapei; Shuster, David L.; Borlina, Cauȇ S.; Nichols, Claire I. O.; Weiss, Benjamin P. (January 3, 2020). [PMID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMID_\(identifier\)) [31911941](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31911941). However, paleomagnetic measurements of Apollo samples indicate that the Moon once generated a core dynamo with surface field intensities of several tens of microtesla (comparable to that of Earth today) during the period 4.25 to 3.56 billion years (Ga) ago (2–7). Following this high-field epoch, the field declined by at least an order of magnitude by 3.2 Ga ago (8) and persisted in a weakened state (\~5 μT) until at least 2.5 Ga ago (9).

[^cite_note-450]: &#x20;"It seems possible, though not certain, that after the conquest Mehmed took over the crescent *and* star as an emblem of sovereignty from the Byzantines. The half-moon alone on a blood red flag, allegedly conferred on the Janissaries by Emir Orhan, was much older, as is demonstrated by numerous references to it dating from before 1453. But since these flags lack the star, which along with the half-moon is to be found on Sassanid and Byzantine municipal coins, it may be regarded as an innovation of Mehmed. It seems certain that in the interior of Asia tribes of Turkish nomads had been using the half-moon alone as an emblem for some time past, but it is equally certain that crescent and star *together* are attested only for a much later period. There is good reason to believe that old Turkish and Byzantine traditions were combined in the emblem of Ottoman and, much later, present-day Republican Turkish sovereignty." Franz Babinger (William C. Hickman Ed., Ralph Manheim Trans.), *Mehmed the Conqueror and His Time*, Princeton University Press, 1992, p 108
